Drill and instruction in the fundamental processes are definitely associated with real-life activity. (See, for example, Addition, p. 4; Subtraction, p. 9; Multiplication, p. 15.)

Each procedure is clearly explained and illustrated. 

Exercises in applying each principle follow each explanation and illustration.

Assignments for pupil to solve unaided by teacher occur at the end of each topical subdivision. 

Part II consists entirely of drill and review problems. 

Arithmetic related to business activities is the feature of Part III. It lays the foundation for the consumer's use of arithmetic. (See p. 291, The Retail Merchant; p. 306, The 

Wholesale Merchant; p. 316, The Manufacturer; p. 321, The Commission Business; p. 329, The 

Banking Business; p. 342, Farming).

NOTICE PARTICULARLY the clear, interesting, easy-tounderstand language.

This book contains an intensive, comparatively simple treatment of arithmetic essentials for students in early high-school grades.

It is also recommended for schools or institutions offering business training.

THIS PREFACE 

HIS work has been prepared to meet the need for a practical treatise on the minimum essentials of arithmetic, as applied in many of our more common social and economic activities. 

The text material of Elements of Practical Arithmetic is divided into three parts. Part I consists of 136 exercises for classwork on the different topics presented by the text, and 56 assignments for the pupils to prepare. The assignments carry on in logical order the development of the lessons and the exercises, and serve as a review of the work covered up to a given point. Part II consists of 27 drills and 30 review assignments. The drills contain exercises for the purpose of developing skill, speed, and accuracy; the assignments contain problems for the purpose of review and of testing the pupil's ability to comprehend and solve simple, practical problems which are commonly met in everyday life. 

Part III, under the heading "Arithmetic in Business," consists of the application of arithmetic to business activities. 

Problems are applied in a related way to such activities as retailing, wholesaling, manufacturing, commission, banking, and farming; and to such other activities as general buying, buying a home, buying a store building, buying on the deferred payment plan, and investing money.

The purpose of Part III is to show the student just how arithmetic is connected with our many business activities. This type of work is intended to broaden the pupil's experience and to give him a better understanding of arithmetic as it is applied in practice. 

The main features of the text can be summed up as follows: 

1. All methods are treated fully. 

2. Basic business information is given so that the source of the many problems can be understood and the problem material more practically applied.

vi 

PREFACE 

3. The content material of the exercises is definitely associated with the activities of business, and thereby stresses the vocational objective of the subject. 

4. The problem material is comparatively easy and is concretely related to our social and economic activities. Thus, the text places special emphasis on the consumer's use of the subject. 

5. The range of content material is limited to the extent that intensive study of the essentials is made possible, and ability to work with figures rapidly and accurately can be stressed and developed. 

6. The book contains 1,687 problems, distributed as follows: 

in Part I, 1,309 problems; in Part II, 250 problems; in Part III, 128 problems. The problems, which can be solved easily and quickly, are of such a nature as to make the subject attractive to all types of pupils. 

The author wishes to emphasize, for the benefit of all users of Elements of Practical Arithmetic, that arithmetic involves much more than mere problem solving. It comprehends a thorough understanding of the subject in terms of the many uses which relate it to everyday social and economic activities. Unrelated problem solving has little worth, but the value of a knowledge of arithmetic in a related sense cannot be overestimated. 

THE AUTHOR

PART I METHODS, EXERCISES, AND ASSIGNMENTS

Without arithmetic, the common affairs of life are not to be managed with success.-George Washington. 

FUNDAMENTAL OPERATIONS 

The four fundamental operations in arithmetic are: addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division.

No problem in arithmetic can be solved or a result obtained without making use of one or more of the four fundamental operations. In solving some problems or in securing a certain result, you may use all four operations before the final result is obtained. Therefore, it is necessary for anyone who has much to do with figures to become thoroughly skilled in the four fundamental operations. $1.20 1.40 1.25 .75 1.12 .84 .54 24 Addition 

COLUMN ADDITION Example. Find the total amount of the following pur chases of groceries: tea, $1.20; coffee, $1.40; sugar, $1.25; flour, $.75; butter, $1.12; cheese, $.84; prunes, $.54; soap, $.24.

In column addition, place numbers directly under one another according to denomination (units under units, tens under tens, and hundreds under hundreds; or cents under cents, dollars under dollars, and so on), and group as many figures as possible when adding. For example, in the first column, group in this manner: (4 and 4) 8, (4 and 2) 14, (5 and 5) 24. In the second column: (2 to carry, 2 and 5) 9, (8 and 1) 18, (7 and 2) 27, (4 and 2) 33. And $7.34 so on.

Grouping figures when adding is a real aid in developing ability to add quickly and accurately.

CHECKING RESULTS IN ADDITION 

Casting out the 9's. Addition results can be easily checked for correctness by casting out the 9's, as shown in the accompanying illustration. $ 8,364-3 5,926-4 7,342-7 

Example. Take the first number, 8,364, and add from left to right: 83+6+421. Then divide the result by 9. (2192, with 3 remaining.) Place the 3 in position (see illustration). Treat each of the other numbers in the same way, placing the remainders in position. Add the remainders, and divide the sum by 9. Treat the answer in the same way that the other numbers have been $37,502-26-8 treated, and if, after the 9's are cast out, the remainder in the answer is the same as the remainder in the problem, the answer is undoubt- 5,821-7 3,762-0 6,287-5 edly correct. (The key number in the illustration is 8.

)

Adding columns both ways. Another way of checking results in addition is to add each column both ways. 

Add all the columns upward, and place the results to the right; then add all the columns downward, and place the results to the left; for the total sum, add the results of the separate columns. See the accompanying illustration.

Addition is the most important and the most frequently used of the four fundamental operations. Anyone who can add rapidly and accurately can, as a rule, perform the other fundamental operations equally well. More trouble in working with figures results from inaccuracy in the fundamental operations than from any other cause.

Subtraction 

By one method, subtraction is a process of finding how much must be added to a certain number to make the sum equal to a certain other number. For example: How much must be added to 36 to make the sum equal 45? The number added, which is 9 (36 + 9 = 45), is the difference between 45 and 36 (45 - 36 9).

This method is used in making change. For example: The amount of the purchase is $3.80, and the customer presents a $10 bill in payment. The salesman will count out, beginning with

the amount of the purchase: $3.80, $3.90, $4.00, $5.00, $10.00 ($3.80+ $6.20 $10.00). To make the change, the salesman will use two dimes, a $1 bill, and a $5 bill ($6.20). 

When you make purchases for cash, observe how the change is counted out.

By another method, subtraction is the process of finding the difference between two numbers, or of finding how much remains when one number is taken from another number. 

Example. 

2,962 -1,784 1,178 Proof. 1,784 +1,178 2,962 

PROVING SUBTRACTION 

The sum of the remainder and the number taken from the larger number will equal the larger number. Thus, to prove a problem in subtraction, such as 94 56 38, add 38 and 56 to see if the sum is 94.

This record for June may be taken as a continuation of the record for May, with the balance carried down for May used as the balance on hand June 1; or a new account may be prepared.

Personal accounts with persons and with business firms are kept by a company for the purpose of showing whether they owe the company or the company owes them. If the left side of the account is larger, they owe the company; if the right side of the account is larger, the company owes them.

CHECKING RESULTS IN MULTIPLICATION 

Casting out the 9's.

Example. Multiply 673 by 87. 

673-7 The remainders, after casting out the 9's in 673 and 87, are 7 and 6. The remainder, after casting out the 

9's in the product of 7 and 6 (7 × 6 42), is 6. The remainder, after casting out the 

9's in the product 58,551, is 6. Therefore the result is, in all probability, correct. 87-6-42--6 58,551-6 Interchanging numbers (multiplier and multiplicand). This is one of the most commonly used methods of checking multiplication results.

Division 

Division is the process of finding how many times one number is contained in another number. It is the reverse of multiplication. 

Example. Divide 192 by 4. In this problem, 192 is the dividend, 4 is the divisor, and 48 is the quotient.

Division is also a short process of subtraction. 

Example. If 48 is subtracted from 192 four times, the last result will be zero (0). 192

AIDS TO MENTAL CALCULATIONS 

The difference between a number and the unit of the next higher order is called the complement of that number. 

Example. The complement of 7 is 3, because 3 is the difference between 7 and 10, 10 being the unit of the next higher order.

AIDS TO MENTAL CALCULATIONS 

19 Likewise, 4 is the complement of 96, because 4 is the difference between 96 and 100, 100 being the unit of the next higher order. 

The complement of the number can be used to advantage when that number is to be taken from the sum of several numbers, particularly when an adding machine is being used. 475.90 $ 143.60 Example. Add $143.60, $312.35, $475.90, and

Following are some practical applications of the simple fundamental processes in arithmetic.

GENERAL PROBLEMS 

Assignment 1 

1. A miller has in storage 162,360 bushels of wheat. If he makes 

3,960 bushels into flour each day, how many days will his supply of wheat last? 

2. In a certain elementary school the cost of books and supplies was $3,872. If there were 484 pupils in the school, what was the average cost of books and supplies per pupil? 

3. In 156 loads of cement, there were 1,026,480 pounds. Find the average number of pounds in each load.

GENERAL PROBLEMS 

21 

4. A company, operating chain stores, purchased 27,560 bushels of potatoes. The potatoes were sent out in equal quantities to 212 stores. 

How many bushels did each store receive? 

5. If it cost $5,876,079 to build 246 miles of railroad, how much did it cost per mile? Prove your result. He 

6. A dealer wishes to ship 20,300 crates of apples to a customer. is informed that 116 crates can be moved in one truck load. How many truck loads will the shipment include? 

7. The amount raised in a certain community chest drive was $59,554.40. If there were 5,134 contributors, how much was the average contribution? 

8. In a certain city there were 9,266 pupils attending the public schools. If the cost of running the schools for one year amounted to $1,213,846, how much was the cost per pupil? 

Assignment 2 

1. A dealer bought 10 crates of eggs, each containing 30 dozen, for which he paid $8.40 a crate. He sold the eggs for 35¢ a dozen. How much did he gain? 

2. A merchant bought 12 boxes of oranges, containing 16 dozen each, for $4.80 a box. He sold the oranges for 40¢ a dozen. How much did he gain? 

3. A truckman contracted to haul 600 loads of coal at $2.25 per load. 

If it took him 50 days to haul the 600 loads, how much did he earn per day? 

4. A dealer bought 5 bunches of bananas for $1.75 a bunch. From the 5 bunches he sold, in all, 55 dozen bananas at 25¢ a dozen. How much did he make? 

5. The expenses of a certain school were as follows: teachers' salaries, $24,800.00; books, $587.50; supplies, $742.25; janitor service, $1,250.00; fuel, $550.00; incidental expenses, $395.25. There were 275 pupils enrolled. Find the average cost per pupil of running the school. 

6. The yearly expenses of a family of four persons were as follows: rent, $600; food, $780; clothing, $412; amusements, $72; fuel, $120; incidentals, $140. Find the average monthly expenses of the family. 

7. A dealer bought 3 bunches of bananas for $3 a bunch. He sold all the bananas on the 3 bunches, amounting to 114 pounds, at 10¢ a pound. How much did he gain?

8. A dealer bought 850 barrels of apples at $3.75 a barrel, and sold them for $5.25 a barrel. If he paid 40¢ a barrel to have them delivered to his customers, how much did he gain? 

Assignment 3 1. The population of a certain city was 28,650, and the cost of running the city government for one year was $161,872.50. Find the per capita cost of the city government. 

2. A jobber bought 20,000 pounds of rice, in New Orleans, for 4¢ a pound. He paid out for the shipment: freight to New York, $140; cartage, $50; insurance, $10. For how much a pound must he sell the rice to gain $200? 

3. A merchant's sales for one week were as follows: Monday, $512.70; 

Tuesday, $607.80; Wednesday, $762.35; Thursday, $988.85; Friday, $598.60; Saturday, $1,040.26. What was the amount of his average daily sales? 

4. A merchant bought 1,288 yards of cloth for $1.25 per yard, and sold from the entire quantity bought 1,282 yards-1,288 yards less 

6 yards wasted in cutting-for $1.65 per yard. How much did he gain? 

5. A young man who was employed as a bank clerk paid $7.50 a week for meals, $3.00 for a room, $1.50 for laundry, and $1.20 for car fare. His clothes and incidental items cost him $313.60 for the year. 

If he saved $300.00 in one year, how much was his salary per week? 

6. A painter contracted to paint a house for $395.00. He paid $3.50 a gallon for 12 gallons of paint, and $6.75 for other materials to use on the job. He employed 4 painters at $8.50 each per day for 7 days.

He paid $25.20 liability insurance on the painters while they were working. How much did he make on this contract? 

7. A dealer bought 500 boxes of apples at 90¢ a box. He paid $125 freight, and $25 cartage. He desired to make a profit of 25¢ on each box. For how much a box must he sell the apples? 

8. A dealer bought 2 carloads of potatoes, containing 1,860 bushels, for 95¢ a bushel, and sold them for $1.40 a bushel. If he paid $216.40 freight and it cost him 10¢ a bushel to handle the potatoes, how much did he make?

Excel Food Choppers FACTORING 

The factors of 21 are 3 and 7, because 3 and 7 when multiplied together equal 21. Those numbers which multiplied together make a number are the factors of that number. 

What are the factors of 8? 15? 24? 35? 56?

A number not exactly divisible by any number except itself and 1 is a prime number. Thus, 5 is a prime number. 

Prime numbers are prime factors.

A number composed of two or more integral factors other than itself and 1 is a composite number. Thus, 14 is a composite number (2 X 7 = 14).

FACTORING 

25 Now, 2 and 8 are factors of 16, but they are not its prime factors. 

The prime factors are 2, 2, 2, and 2 (2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 16). By the factors of a number are usually meant its prime factors. 

A number may have two or more sets of factors other than its prime factors. Thus, the factors of 28 are 4 and 7, or 2 and 14. 

To find the prime factors of a number.

Example. Find the prime factors of 4,284. 

22,142 31,071 

The prime factors of 4,284 are 2, 2, 3, 3, 7, and 17 

24,284 (2 × 2 × 3 × 3 X 7 X 17 = 4,284). The large factors are 36 and 119, or 252 and 17. Unless a person has access to factor tables, the only method of determining large factors is by trying the prime numbers until the right one is found. To find the prime factors, always divide by the smallest number possible. 3 357 119 17 Exercise 19

The factor method can be used in some cases to simplify the process in multiplication or division. By using the factor method it is possible at times to make many of the computations mentally. 

Example. 

472 472 63 1416 2832 29736 29736 

Multiplying by the Factor Method 

Multiply 472 by 63. 

The factors of 63 are 7 and 9. Multiplying 

472 by 9, and then by 7, gives the same result as 

4248 multiplying 472 by 63. The factor method can be used to advantage at times to prove multiplication results.

CANCELLATION 

Cancellation is a method of shortening the process of multiplying fractions, dividing fractions, or of finding results in many other business computations.

To find results by means of cancellation. 

Examples. 

1. Find the result of 36 X 72 108 

Cancel 36 into 108; the result is 3. Cancel 3 into 72; the result is 24. Hence the final result is 24. 24 36 × 72 == 24 108 24 X 8 X 36 

2. Find the result of 12 X 4 X 9 Cancel the numbers below the line into the numbers above the line. The result is 2 X 2 X 4, or 16. 2 2 4 

24 × 8 × 36 

12 × 4 × 9 

3. Find the result of 36 X 48 27 2 X 2 X 4 = 16 The factor 9 is contained in 36 four times, and in 27 three times. The 3 is contained in 48 sixteen times. Therefore the result is 4 X 16, or 64. 4 16 36 × 48 4 X 16 64 27

FRACTIONS 

Any quantity less than a unit, or less than one, is a fraction of that unit; therefore, a fraction is a part of a whole, or of a unit. 50¢ 25¢ dollar dollar A fraction is composed of a numerator, which is written above, and a denominator, which is written below, a horizontal line. 

Thus, is a fraction. This means that the whole quantity, or the unit, is divided into four equal parts, as shown by the denominator, and that three of the parts, as shown by the numerator, are taken; therefore, of the whole quantity is taken. 

Common fractions are classified as simple, compound, and complex; as proper and improper; and as parts of mixed numbers. 

A simple fraction is one whose numerator and denominator are whole numbers. Thus is a simple fraction. 

A compound fraction is a fraction of a fraction. Thus, of is a compound fraction. 

A complex fraction is one in which either the numerator or the denominator, or both are not whole numbers. Thus, the following are complex fractions: 2 12 23 31

FRACTIONS 

29 A proper fraction is one in which the numerator is less than the denominator. Thus,,, and are proper fractions. 

An improper fraction is one in which the numerator equals or exceeds the denominator. Thus, 8, 15, and 2 are improper fractions. 24 A mixed number is a whole number and a fraction taken together. Thus, 21, 43, and 62 are mixed numbers. 

Fractions are used so extensively in business computations that fundamentally there is no part of arithmetic of more importance than that part which has to do with fractions.

DECIMAL FRACTIONS 

A decimal fraction is expressed in tenths, hundredths, thousandths, and so forth.

In decimal fractions, the denominator is not written as in common fractions; instead, it is indicated by means of a decimal point. Thus, one tenth () is written .1; three tenths (1) is written .3; seven hundredths (100) is written .07; twenty-five hundredths (0) is written .25; eighteen thousandths (118) is written .018.

A decimal is either simple or complex. A simple decimal has a whole number written to the right of the decimal point; as, .25, .175, .06375. A complex decimal has a whole number and a common fraction written to the right of the decimal point; as, .8, .37, .0473.

ALIQUOT PARTS

ALIQUOT PARTS 

An aliquot part of a number is a part that will be contained in that number without leaving a remainder. Thus, 2 is an aliquot part of 10; 16 is an aliquot part of 100; 33 is an aliquot part of 100. 

Any multiple of an exact divisor of a number is also an aliquot part of that number. Thus, 663, or twice (33), is an aliquot part of 100.

The aliquot parts of 100, such as halves, thirds, fourths, fifths, sixths, eights, twelfths, and sixteenths are the ones most commonly used in business.

Table of Aliquot Parts 

The following table contains the most common parts of 100.

How to Use Aliquot Parts 

Aliquot parts can be used in finding the cost of commodities sold by the yard, pound, gallon, bushel, or any like measure.

Example. What is the cost of 45 yards of cloth at 33¢ a yard? 33¢ = }} of $1 

45 yd. at $1 of $45 $45 $15, the cost 1 Therefore, 45 yards at $($.33) cost $15. 

Consider the articles listed as costing $1 each, and take such a fractional part of this amount as the aliquot part is of $1. Using aliquot parts simplifies the process so that many of the computations can be made mentally. 

The number of articles and the price can be interchanged as a means of simplifying the process. Thus, 37 yards at $2.40 a yard can be interchanged to 240 yards at 37½¢. $240 

240 yd. at $1 

240 × $3 ($.37) = $90

When using aliquot parts in computations, refer to the table on page 51. 

When the table of aliquot parts is not available-to find the fraction equivalent for an aliquot part of 100, place the aliquot part over 100, and reduce the result to a simple fraction. 

Find what aliquot part .41 is of 100.

Example. 

4123 125 

100 300 12' the aliquot part When aliquot parts are used in computations, the process may sometimes be simplified by breaking up the larger number into smaller units. The smaller fractions make it easy to perform mentally many operations that would otherwise be more difficult. 

For example, .87 can be divided into 1, 1, and 1, which, when added together, are equal to . Likewise, .83, or, can be divided into and 1.

Clerkships

In large business organizations engaged in buying and selling merchandise, there are many divisions of work and responsibility. Such divisions are usually designated by different clerkships. The clerkships most frequently found in business organizations are: receiving clerk, order clerk, billing clerk, stock clerk or inventory clerk, shipping clerk, and pay roll clerk. All of these different clerkships entail some knowledge of arithmetic, and accuracy is of the greatest importance. 

The receiving clerk receives the goods and checks them in, with the invoices or bills received. The order clerk receives and books the orders for goods sold. 

The work of the other clerks is described in the following text. 

Sometimes two or three clerkships are combined into one if the volume of business does not warrant the employment of more people. 

WORK OF THE BILL CLERK 

The work of the bill clerk in merchandising commodities of different kinds is to prepare the invoices of the goods sold. 

The invoice is an itemized list of all articles sold, together with the price of each article, the cost of each purchase, and the total cost of the shipment. The invoice contains also: the place and date of purchase, the names of the seller and the buyer, and the terms of purchase. An invoice is sometimes called a bill, and, in the hands of the purchaser, is commonly referred to as a "bill."

An order for goods is taken by a salesman, a sales clerk, or an order clerk. Sometimes the invoice, or bill, is made out by the person who takes the order, but in large organizations several bill clerks are kept busy preparing invoices. It is necessary for clerks to be extremely accurate, for mistakes in invoices often cause much trouble.

Orders for goods come in to the order clerk or the bill clerk either from salesmen or direct from customers, who may call personally to place the order, telephone it in, or send it in by mail. 

When the order is received, the goods are sent as directed and the invoice is prepared by someone, usually the bill clerk, and mailed. to the customer.

WORK OF THE STOCK CLERK 

The stock clerk checks in new stock, checks out stock requested on requisitions, keeps stock records up-to-date, and at certain times prepares an inventory of stock on hand. It is the practice to inventory goods at the cost price. Sometimes allowance is made for old styles, shopworn or damaged goods, and unusual price changes. Keeping a record of stock in a manufacturing or merchandising establishment is a task that entails responsibility as well as accuracy.

Perpetual stock records are written up at regular intervals, usually each month, from receipts and sales records compiled during that month. The blank record sheet is usually large enough to contain the records for twelve months, and at the end of this period the inventory is completed to show the total value of the stock on hand.

CALCULATING PARCEL POST CHARGES 

To find parcel post charges. Postal Information contains a table of parcel post charges, for each zone, on parcels weighing from 1 to 70 pounds. Anyone shipping by parcel post regularly should secure a copy of this table. 

However, by use of the table of rates on page 63, parcel post charges may be determined as follows: 

Example. Find the cost of sending a package, valued at $25 and weighing 7 pounds, into the fifth zone. 

The rate is 11¢ for the first pound, and 5.3¢ a pound for each additional pound. The insurance rate is 10¢ for $25 valuation. 

11¢, rate for first pound 

32¢, rate for additional 6 pounds at 5.3¢ per pound 

10¢, insurance on $25 valuation 

53¢, total cost

The method of finding parcel post charges can be shortened by multiplying the number of pounds by the rate for additional pounds and by adding to this result the difference between the rate for the first pound and the rate for additional pounds. 

Example. Find the cost of sending a package, weighing 21 pounds, into the fourth zone.

73.5¢, rate for 21 pounds at 3.5¢ per pound 

6.5, (10é, for first pound - 3.5¢) 

80.0¢, total cost

SHIPPING BY FREIGHT 

Freight shipments are handled by railroad companies, by steamship companies, and by trucking companies or individual truckmen; all such agencies are classified generally as common carriers.

The shipping clerk must be acquainted with the many different terms used in connection with freight shipments; he must understand classifications of rates, of territories, and of articles offered for shipment. His duties include: marking the articles to be shipped; weighing shipments; making out the bill of lading for each shipment; and, in some cases, specifying the routing of the shipment.

The terms used in shipping are: consignor, consignee, straight bill of lading, order bill of lading, waybill, freight rate, freight classification, commodity rate, class rate, freight bill, territory classification, and freight tariff.

The consignor is the one who ships, or consigns, something. 

The consignee is the one to whom something is shipped. 

The bill of lading is a document, issued in triplicate, by which a railroad or other transportation company acknowledges the receipt of goods to be shipped. The bill of lading contains shipping directions and an agreement on the part of the transportation company to deliver the goods to the consignee. 

Bills of lading are issued in triplicate, by means of carbon paper. 

The set of three copies includes: the first copy, headed Original; the second, Shipping Order; and the third, Memorandum. The original is signed by the agent for the carrier and by the consignor, and is sent to the consignee. The shipping order is signed by the consignor and is retained by the agent for the carrier. The memorandum is signed by the agent for the carrier and by the consignor, and is kept by the latter. 

The straight bill of lading is the form most commonly used. 

It is not negotiable; that is, it cannot be transferred, by indorsement, to a third party.

The order bill of lading is used when goods are to be paid for before delivery, or at a stated (in writing) future date; or

when the bill of lading is to be transferred to another party, as assignee.

A waybill is a document, issued in duplicate, for the purpose of giving the routing of the shipment. The first copy is kept by the agent for the carrier at the place from which the goods are shipped, and the second copy is sent to the agent for the carrier at the destination of the goods. 

The freight rate is the transportation charge, usually per hundred pounds, according to freight classification: first class, second class, third class, fourth class, or fifth class.

Freight classification is based on the kind of commodity to be shipped, its weight, and the distance it is to be transported. 

The two kinds of freight rates are: commodity rates and class rates.

Commodity rates are rates charged on articles shipped in large quantities, such as grain, coal, lumber, and the like. 

Class rates are rates charged on different articles according to classes shown in the freight-classification lists. 

Trucking companies do not at this time have a uniform schedule of rates; to some extent they base their charges on the number of units carried-bushels, barrels, baskets, boxes, and so on-rather than on the weight of the shipment. The A freight bill is a bill for the freight charges on a shipment. 

Territory classification is based upon the division of the United States into districts as a means of adjusting freight rates. territory classifications are: Official, Western, and Southern.

The freight tariff is a book containing the following information: names of shipping points (railroad stations), classifications of articles, and freight rates. 

Any local freight office will give full information on shipping by freight.

CALCULATING FREIGHT CHARGES 

To find freight charges.

Example. Find the freight charges on 6 cases of dry goods sent from Boston, Mass., to Albany, N. Y. The cases weigh 212, 221,

215, 226, 208, and 196 pounds, respectively. The first class freight rate is 64¢ per 100 pounds. 

212 lb. + 221 lb. + 215 lb. + 226 lb. + 208 lb. + 196 lb. 

1,278 lb. 

1,278 lb. = 12.78 cwt. 

12.78 × 64¢ = $8.18 

The total weight is 1,278 pounds, or 12.78 hundredweight. If the freight on 1 hundredweight is 64¢, the freight on 12.78 hundredweight will be 12.78 multiplied by 64.

SHIPPING BY EXPRESS 

Express shipping service is used for shipping articles that must be delivered quickly or that are not suitable for shipment by freight on account of the way in which freight is handled. Articles that are valuable or fragile (easily broken or damaged) are

shipped by express. This service includes calling for the article at the place from which it is to be shipped, and delivering the article to the consignee at its destination. 

There are about 300 different scales of express rates, each one designated by a block number. The scale of rates under which the shipment comes depends on distance; on the valuation, weight, and size of the article to be shipped; and on whether the article is fragile or perishable. Rates The express rate is usually a fixed charge per pound, or per hundred pounds, according to scale and classification. are classified as first class, second class, and third class.

When the article shipped weighs less than one hundred pounds, the rate per pound is higher proportionately. The more the article weighs, up to one hundred pounds, the less proportionately is the rate.

Express rates include insurance up to $50 on packages weighing one hundred pounds or less. Packages weighing more than one hundred pounds may be insured for their full value if a small additional fee is paid. The amount of insurance is not limited. 

Express packages may be sent charges collect, and the packages may also be sent C. O. D. For the latter service the express company makes a charge for collecting the C. O. D. amount from the consignee and for remitting it to the consignor. 

Express companies will give full information to anyone interested in using their service.

CALCULATING EXPRESS CHARGES 

To find express charges. 

Example. What are the express charges on 5 packages of electrical appliances, weighing 245 pounds, shipped from Schenectady, N. Y., to Boston, Mass.? The agent for the American

Railway Express Company at Schenectady, after referring to the Rate Table and Block Tariff Sheet (a publication issued by the express company) and deciding on the classification, finds the rate to be $1.45 per 100 pounds. 2.45 X $1.45 $3.55, the charges

Pay Rolls

WORK OF THE PAY ROLL CLERK 

The pay roll clerk must keep a complete record of each employee's time and absence, in order to compute the amount of wages to be paid to each employee; he must prepare the pay roll for a definite period of time, usually a week, two weeks, or one month; he must secure from the bank the necessary funds to pay each employee the amount due him, or he must prepare the salary checks for the employees. These checks are usually signed by the treasurer of the firm or some other authorized official. 

When the money for wages is drawn from the bank in one amount, to be paid out in cash, an officer of the firm or the proprietor will sign a pay roll check, made out for the full amount. 

The pay roll clerk must be acquainted with his employer's plans and policies concerning overtime, extra allowances, employ-

ees' expense items, insurance, deductions or allowances of pay in case of absence, and also plans for employees' vacations with pay and without pay.

A pay roll is a record of the time each employee has worked during a certain period, usually a week, two weeks, or a month; the rate of pay; and the amount of wages to be paid. If the employees do piece work, the record shows, over a period of time, the number of pieces completed by each worker, the rate of pay, the amount to be paid to each worker, and the total amount of wages to be paid out.

Employees are paid by the hour, day, week, or month. When workers are paid by the hour, the records may show fractions of an hour: halves, quarters, or tenths, and sometimes exact minutes, if the employer requires such accuracy. Piece-rate employees are also paid by the piece-by the dozen, by the hundred, or by the thousand pieces-according to the articles made and the prevailing practice followed.

TIME RECORDING 

In job work or piece work, there is usually some device for recording the employee's time or for keeping a record of the work completed. Usually the record is kept on a time card; in the case of piece work, the information is kept on a record sheet, or card. Very often some mechanical device, such as a recording stamp or a time clock, is used for recording each employee's time. 

Where the dial recorder is used, the number given each employee is punched on the dial, and this number together with the time "In" and "Out" is recorded daily on a strip of paper. 

This record is removed at the end of a certain period, when the pay roll is made up from the dial record. 

Where the time clock is used, a record card, similar to the form on page 74, is kept. This card is placed in the machine each time the employee comes in or goes out, and the time is automatically stamped on the card. Many different forms of record. cards are in common use.

This type of card is used where all employees arrive and depart on the same regular schedule.

Dial Recorder. 

Example. On the card illustrated at the top of page 74, time is reckoned to the nearest quarter hour. Verify the time Barnes worked this particular week. 

Suppose that regular time, at the rate of 8 hours per day, averaged 48 hours per week. If Barnes were paid for overtime at the rate of 1 times regular time, what would be his total wages for the week?

PAY ROLL CHECK 

The pay roll check, illustrated on page 77, is made out and, together with the pay roll slip, is taken to the bank for the purpose of securing the necessary funds with which to pay each employee his wages. When the check is cashed by the bank, the money received is taken to the employer's place of business, and the amount due each employee is put into a small envelope to be given to each employee at the appointed time.

This check, made payable to "Pay Roll," is payable to the bearer. The bank, however, will require the person presenting the check to indorse it before it is cashed.

Reading Meters 

Utility companies furnishing gas, electricity, and water install meters which register the quantity of gas, electric current, or water consumed. These meters are read, by the utility company furnishing the service, at regular intervals-usually of one month. 

If it so happens that a meter reader's schedule calls for the reading of a particular meter on the tenth of the month, he will schedule this particular meter to be read on the tenth of each month. following. This time will vary only one or two days on account of Sundays and holidays.

There are times when it is important for consumers of metered services to know how to read meters; moreover, any employee in the service of a utility company may be charged with the duty of reading and checking meters for a consumer. Therefore it is advisable to know how to read the meters in common use. 

GAS METERS 

Gas furnished to the consumer is run through a gas meter, which measures the number of cubic feet of gas consumed. 10,000 2/2 1,000 

Gas Meter Reading. Set 1a. 

100

The price charged for gas consumed is a certain rate per 1,000 cubic feet.

The dials on this gas meter registered 4,420 cubic feet when read on June 15, as shown in the reading Set la. (See page 81.)

The dials on the same gas meter registered 5,540 cubic feet, as shown in the reading Set 1b, when read on July 15. These readings indicate that 1,120 cubic feet (5,540 4,420) were consumed. 

10,000 2 2 1,000 100 8/8 3 3 

Gas Meter Reading. Set 1b. 

When a gas meter is read, the right-hand dial is read from left to right; the center dial, from right to left; and the left-hand dial, from left to right. The numbers above the dials-10,000, 1,000, and 100, in the illustrations-denote the number of cubic feet recorded by a complete revolution of the hands on their respective dials. The left-hand dial registers the number of cubic feet up to ten thousand; the center dial, up to one thousand; and the right-hand dial, up to one hundred. A meter may contain as many as six dials. (See illustration on page 84.)

To read a gas meter. Begin with the right-hand dial (see 

Set 1a). Mark down the registered number. Its position in your answer-tens, hundreds, thousands, and so on-is determined by the dial on which it appears. The number registered is the number at which the hand on the dial directly points, or has passed on its way to the next number. The number on the dial should not be read until the hand is fully over or has passed

it. Continue to the left, reading each dial in order and placing each number in its correct position. 

Refer to the reading Set 1a. The right-hand dial registers 

2+. As this dial registers up to 100, the 2 is read 20. Each division of this dial is equal to 10. Each division of the center dial is 100; and of the left-hand dial, 1,000. Therefore, this meter reads 20, 420, 4,420, in order, respectively, as the numbers are placed in position.

ELECTRIC METERS 

Dials on this electric meter registered 3,328 on June 15, and registered 3,573 on July 15. Subtracting (3,573 - 3,328) indicates that 245 kilowatt-hours were consumed. 2 10000 1,000 100 10 2 

Electric Meter. Last Reading, July 15. 

The unit of measurement expressed in reading an electric meter is the kilowatt-hour, abbreviated kw.-hr. 

The dial on the right registers the single kilowatt-hours up to ten; the next one, to the left, up to one hundred; the next, up to one thousand; and the one on the left, up to ten thousand. 

When an electric meter is read, the single-hour and the hundredhour dials are read from left to right, and the ten-hour and the thousand-hour dials are read from right to left.

To read an electric meter. In reading an electric meter, begin with the right-hand dial, and proceed as in reading a gas meter. Exercise 56 The following readings show what an electric meter registered on four different dates, at intervals one month apart.

POWERS AND ROOTS 

Powers A power of a number is the product obtained by multiplying a number by itself any number of times. Thus, 4 X 4 = 16; 

16 is the second power of 4. Again, 4 X 4 X 4 64; 64 is the third power of 4. And, 4 X 4 X 4 X 4 = 256; 256 is the fourth power of 4.

The small number written to the right and a little above the number to be raised to a certain power is called the exponent. 

Thus, 62 means that 6 is to be raised to its second power (6 × 6 36); 63 means that 6 is to be raised to its third power 

The second power is called the square of the number; the third power is called the cube of the number. Other higher powers are denoted by their respective exponents; for example, 85 is read the fifth power of 8.

To calculate higher powers of a whole number. 

Example. Find the third power of 9. 

9 X 9 X 9 = 729 

Therefore, the third power of 9 (93) is 729. 

1. Find the square of 16. 

2. Find the cube of 8.

Exercise 57 

3. Find the fourth power of 12. 

4. Find the square of 48. 

5. Find the cube of 24. 

6. Find the fifth power of 5.

Roots The root of a number is that number which was multiplied by itself two or more times to produce a certain number or power. 

Thus, 4 is the square root of 16 (4 X 4 = 16); 6 is the cube root of 216 (6 X 6 X 6 216); 5 is the fourth root of 625 (5 X 5 X 

5 X 5 625). The root of a number is always one of the equal factors of that number. The radical sign (√¯¯¯¯), when placed above a number, indicates that the root of that number is to be found. Thus, cates that the square root is to be found (√36 = 6). 

To find the square root of a whole number. 36 indi- Example. What is the length of one side of a square lot, the area of which is 15,625 sq. ft.?

125 11.56.25 201 22 56 240 44 245 12.25 12.25 

Beginning at the right, separate the number into groups of two figures each.

Find the largest perfect square which may be taken from the left-hand group (1).

Subtract the square of the root already found, and bring down the next group (56). 

Double the root already found (1 + 1 (annex 0 = 20), and divide. 

2), annex a cipher

Add the root in the quotient (2) to the trial divisor (2 + 20 

22), and multiply by that root (22 X 2 = 44). 

Subtract as before, and bring down the next group (12.25). 

Double the root already found (12 + 12 = 24), annex a cipher (annex 0 240), and divide. 

The final result, or the square root of 15,625, is 125. To prove the result, multiply 125 by 125 (125 × 125 15,625).

MEASUREMENTS IN USE 

Practical measurements form the basis of much of our study of arithmetic, because measurements are fundamental features of many of our business and industrial activities. Hence, measure-

ments are emphasized as one of the most important parts of practical arithmetic.

Square Measure 

Square measure is used principally to find the surface areas of objects when the length and width are given. 

A surface has length and breadth, but not thickness. 

A rectangle is a plane figure having four right angles. 

A square is a rectangle having four equal sides. 

To find the area of a rectangle when the length and width are given.

Examples. 

1. Find the number of square feet in the surface area of a table top 4 ft. square. 4 X 4 

16, the number of sq. ft.

Therefore, there are 16 sq. ft. in the area of the top of this table, which corresponds to the figure of the square illustrated. 6 ft. 4ft. 4 ft. 4 ft. Rectangle. Square. 

2. Find the number of square feet in the surface area of a table top 6 ft. long and 4 ft. wide, as in the figure of the rectangle illustrated. 

6 × 4 

24, the number of sq. ft.

The rule for finding the area of a rectangle may be stated as 

Therefore, the following are also true: 

Width length square units in area 

Area width units of length 

Area length units of width

For the application of these rules, the length and the width must be given in the same units; for example, both dimensions must be in rods, in yards, in feet, or in inches. Exercise 59 1. How many square feet are there in a room 12 ft. square (12 ft. X 

12 ft.)?

To find the perimeter of a rectangle. 

Example. Find the perimeter of a rectangle 4 ft. long and 2 ft. wide. 4 ft.+4 ft. + 2 ft.+2 ft. 

12 ft., the perimeter

The perimeter of any rectangle is the combined length of its four sides. In a square rectangle, the perimeter is equal to four times the length of one side.

64 cu. in.

128 cu. in.

Angles and Triangles 

An angle is an opening between two straight lines that meet at a point.

Angle. 

Right Angle. 

Equilateral Triangle. 

A right angle is an angle measured by an arc equal to one fourth of a circumference, or 90 degrees.

A triangle is a plane figure which has three sides and three angles.

An equilateral triangle has all its sides and all its angles equal. 

A right triangle has one right angle.

Right Triangle. 

ABC Hypotenuse. Altitude. = Base.

The hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle is the side opposite the right angle.

The base is the side on which the triangle stands. 

The altitude is the perpendicular line from the base, upward, to the highest point. 

To find the area of a triangle.

Example. Find the area of a triangle having an altitude of 

8 ft. and a base of 10 ft. 8 X 10 2 

40, the number of sq. .ft. 10 ft. 

A rectangle 8 ft. by 10 ft. would contain 80 sq. ft. A triangle with an altitude of 8 ft. and a base of 10 ft. contains 40 sq. ft., or as many square feet as a rectangle 8 ft. by 10 ft. would contain. 8ft. 10 ft. 

Area of Triangle. 

8ft. 

To find the area of any triangle, find the product of the base and altitude, and divide the result by 2.

Circular Measure THE CIRCLE 

A circle is a plane surface enclosed by a curved line called the circumference, every part of which is the same distance from a point within called the center. 

The diameter is a straight line drawn through the center of the circle from two opposite points on the circumference.

CIRCUMFERENCE DIAMETER 

Circle.

RADIUS The radius is a straight line drawn from any point on the circumference to the center of the circle.

An arc of a circle is any part of the circumference. 

To find the circumference of a circle when the diameter is given. By actual measurement, it has been found that the circumference of a circle is 3.1416 times

Therefore, to find the circumference of a circle when the diameter is given, multiply the diameter by 3.1416. 

Example. Find the circumference of a circle having a diameter of 4 ft. the diameter. 4 ft. X 3.1416 .5664 X 12 in. 

12.5664 ft., the circumference = 6.7968 in., or 63 in., approximately

Therefore, the result is 12 ft. 6 in.

To find the diameter of a circle when the circumference is given. To find the diameter of a circle, divide the circumference by 3.1416. For many practical purposes, 3 may be used instead of 3.1416. 

Example. Find the diameter of a circle having a circumference of 18.8496 ft. 

18.8496 ft. ÷ 3.1416 = 6 ft., the diameter

To find the area of a circle when the diameter is given.

The area of a circle can be found in any of these ways: (a) By multiplying the circumference by one half the radius. (b) By multiplying the square of the diameter by .7854 (4 of 3.1416).

(c) By multiplying the square of the radius (the diameter) by 3.1416.

A solid has three dimensions: length, breadth, and thickness. 

A prism is a solid the bases of which are equal, similar, and parallel plane figures, and the sides of which are parallelograms. Cylinder. 

Rectangular 

Prism. 

A cylinder is a circular body, of uniform diameter, the bases of which are equal parallel circles. 

To find the volume of a rectangular solid. 

Example. Find the volume of a rectangular solid 4 ft. long, 3 ft. wide, and 2 ft. deep. 4 X 3 X 2 = 24 cu. ft.

For each foot of depth, there are 4 × 3 × 1 cu. ft., or 12 cu. ft., in this rectangular solid. For the 2 feet of depth, there are 

2 X 12 cu. ft., or 24 cu. ft. 

Therefore, the length times the width (breadth) times the depth (thickness) equals the volume of a rectangular solid. Exercise 66 1. Find the volume of a cut stone 2 ft. square and 

3 ft. long.

To find the volume of a cylinder. 

Rectangular Solid.

Example. Find the volume of a cylindrical tank the diameter of which is 3 ft. and the height, 4 ft. 3 X 3 X .7854 7.0686 X 4 

7.0686, sq. ft. in area of base 

28.2744, cu. ft. in volume 

For each foot of height, the diameter squared times .7854 equals the volume. For 4 feet of height, the volume is 4 times 

7.0686, or 28.2744 cu. ft. 

Or, the following solution may be used: 1 × 1 2 X 3.1416 7.0686 X 4 = × 1 = ↓ = 21 

7.0686, sq. ft. in area of base 

28.2744, cu. ft. in volume 

For each foot of height, the radius (1 ft.) squared times 3.1416 equals the volume. For 4 feet of height, the volume is 4 times 

7.0686, or 28.2744 cu. ft.

Therefore, the volume of a cylinder is equal to the area of the base times the height.

CONSTRUCTION WORK

CONSTRUCTION WORK 

Construction work, which includes all types of building activity, requires a great amount of figuring. Measurements are used extensively in construction work; measurement problems occur more frequently than any others. The major part of arithmetic in practice has to do with standard units of weight and measure, and the major part of arithmetic used in different building activities has to do with measurements. 

Lumber Measurement 

The unit of lumber measurement is the board foot, which is a square foot of any kind of lumber one inch thick. Lumber less than one inch thick is measured as though it were one inch thick. 

To find the number of board feet in a quantity of lumber.

Examples. 

1. How many board feet are there in 80 pieces of lumber 

2" X 6" X 12' long? 12 X X 2 X 80 12 960, the number of board feet By expressing the width as a fraction over 12 (12 in. reduce the width in inches to feet: 6" 67 = 12 1 ft.), To find the number of board feet in a piece of lumber, multiply the length in feet by the width in feet by the thickness in inches. If there is more than one piece of lumber, multiply by the number of pieces. The cancellation method can be used in the computation. 

2. At $42 per M, find the cost of 60 pieces of lumber 2" X 4" X 18' long. 18 X 4 X 2 X 60 12 720 $42 per M 720, the number of board feet 720 X $42 1000 $30.24, the cost

To find the cost per M, point off three places to the left in the number of board feet, or remove the decimal point three places to the left in that number, and multiply by the price per M. 

Lumber Tables 

Lumber tables are extensively used by lumber merchants. 

A complete table shows the number of board feet, for the different lengths in feet, in all sizes of lumber commonly employed. 

As an illustration, the following table includes a few of the most common sizes.

Many articles of lumber are sold by the piece, such as window sashes and doors. Laths are sold by the bundle of 50 each.

Roofing Measurements 

Roofing material is usually estimated by the square foot or by the square yard, except when shingles or different kinds of shingle materials are used. In estimating for shingle materials, the unit of computation is the square, equal to 100 square feet. 

Example. In the two sides of a roof, 24' x 40' on each side, there would be 19.2 squares. 24 X 40 X 2 100 

19.2, the number of squares 1 To find the number of squares in a roof, divide the number of square feet in the surface of the roof by 100 (100 sq. ft. square).

Shingles are estimated at the uniform width of 4 inches. A bundle of shingles is assumed to contain the equivalent of 250 shingles, each 4 inches wide, although the bundle may be made up of shingles of varying widths. Estimates have to be made in whole bundles, since, as a rule, parts of a bundle are not sold. 

The following scale can be used for estimating the number of shingles necessary to lay one square, under specified conditions: 

TABLE OF SHINGLES REQUIRED PER SQUARE Number of 

Shingles 

Inches Laid to Weather 900.. 800. 720. 655. 4/2 51 600.

The foregoing table may be computed as follows: A square is 

10' X 10', or 100 sq. ft. Multiply the number of square inches in a square foot (144 sq. in.) by 100. Divide the result by the number of square inches in the exposed surface of each shingle. 

Example. How many 4" shingles, laid 4" to the weather, will lay one square? (144 X 100) (4 × 41) 

800, the number of shingles

Therefore, 800 shingles, 4" wide and laid 41" to the weather, are required to lay one square (100 sq. ft.) 

Shingle materials such as metal, asphalt, and slate shingles, and tile roofing-come in uniform widths. 

Example. In laying a slate roof, the slates are 8" wide and laid 6" to the weather. How many slates will lay one square? 

14,400 ÷ 48 (6 × 8) 

300, the number of slates

Therefore, 300 slates, 8" wide and laid 6" to the weather, would lay one square (100 sq. ft.).

To estimate the number of shingles per square when shingle material is of uniform width, find the number of square inches exposed to the weather, and divide 14,400 (144 × 100) by this result.

When any kind of shingle material is used, the first rows laid have to be laid double, to make the roof leak proof. From 50 to 

100 extra shingles per square are usually allowed for double courses or rows, and for waste in laying. These allowances must be considered in estimating amounts and figuring costs. 

To find the cost of roofing material. 

Example. Find the cost, at $4.50 per M, of shingles for a roof 16' x 30' on each side. The shingles are laid 5" to the weather. 16 X 30 X 2 100 30 X 2 X 12

Masonry Measurements 

Masonry estimates are usually by the square foot, the cubic foot, or the cubic yard. The cubic yard is the standard unit employed most frequently in estimates for excavations and for quantities of stone or concrete to be used. 

The brick most commonly employed in brick masonry measures 8" X 4" X 2". To estimate the number of bricks necessary for each cubic foot of masonry, make an allowance, for the mortar, of an additional " on each dimension of the brick.

The number of bricks required for a cubic foot of masonry is calculated as follows: 

1,728 ÷ (81 × 45 × 21) approximately 22 bricks Thus, 1,728 cu. in. (1,728 cu. in. = 1 cu. ft.) divided by the number of cubic inches in a brick (8 × 41 × 21 788 cu. in.), after 1" has been added to each dimension, gives 22 as the nearest whole number of bricks per cubic foot. 

The number of bricks of any size other than standard required for a cubic foot of masonry can be found in the same way. 

To find the number of bricks required to build a piece of masonry. 18ft. 20ft. 38 ft. 40ft.

Liquid Measure 

The gallon contains 231 cu. in. Therefore, a cubic foot of liquid will contain 17, or 7.48 gal. (approximately 7½ gal.). 

To find the number of gallons in a tank.

1,128.0384, number of gal.

To find the approximate capacity of a tank or reservoir, multiply its capacity in cubic feet by 71; or, to be more exact, by 7.48. Drawings made to scale are made so that one inch, or a fraction of an inch, represents one foot, or a number of feet.

PERCENTAGE 

Percentage problems occur frequently in business transactions, particularly in those transactions which require the computing of discounts, commissions, interest, and so on. It is also a common practice to express the relation between two numbers in per cent; for example, the relation of operating costs

to sales, of advertising costs to sales, of selling expense to gross profit on sales, and so forth. "Per cent" means by the hundred, and is a decimal form of computing by hundredths. Per cent is another name for the decimal form "hundredths"; 3 per cent, .03, and all have the same value. 3 The symbol, or sign, for per cent is %. Thus, 8% means .

08, or 180. 

Any per cent can be expressed either as a decimal or as a common fraction, and either form can be used in problem solving. 

For instance, "four hundredths" may be written: as a per cent, 4%; as a decimal, .04; or, as a fraction, T. Fractional equivalents may be used; for example, 4% may be expressed as either or.

The base is the number on which the per cent is computed. 

Thus, in the computation 6% of $100, $100 is the base. 

The rate is the per cent to be taken. In the computation 6% of $100, 6% is the rate.

The percentage is the result obtained by taking as many hundredths of the base as the rate shows. In the computation 

6% of $100, the percentage is $6.

In every percentage problem there are three parts, or elements: base, rate, and percentage. When any two of the parts, or elements, are given, the missing third part, or element, can be found. 

1. Given Base and Rate; find Percentage. 

2. Given Percentage and Base; find Rate. 

3. Given Percentage and Rate; find Base.

To find the percentage when the base and the rate are given. 

Example. Find 12% of $1,520.

Or: 100% 1% $1,520, the base $15.20 (Point off two places for 1%) 

12% 12 $15.20 $182.40, the percentage 12% $1,520 X .12 = .12 

$182.40, the percentage

PERCENTAGE 

123 Hence, the rate (expressed as a decimal) times the base equals the percentage.

Always place to the right of the equality sign in the equation whatever unit is required in the answer; for example, per cent, dollars, pounds, bushels, and the like. Exercise 76

2. Find 23% of $1,780.

6. A concern manufactured and sold $480,000 worth of goods in one year. The advertising costs amounted to 12% of the sales. How much did the concern pay for advertising?

To find the rate when the percentage and the base are given. 

Example. What per cent of $1,500 is $180? 

The following may be the solution: $1 $1,500 100% (Per cent is required in answer)

Charge Accounts 

Charge accounts are frequently used in business transactions; in fact, a large part of the business of buying and selling is done on credit. Credits are handled through charge accounts. When a customer buys goods for which he does not pay at the time of making the purchase, he is charged on the books of the seller (creditor) with the amount of the purchase price of the goods. 

At various intervals, bills or statements are rendered for these accounts. As the bills are paid, the customer receives credit on the books of the seller. When the full amount has been paid, the account is settled, or balanced. 

It is in this connection that cash and trade discounts are featured so extensively. 19JAMES W. FORBES 

216 Milk Street, Boston Mass.

19Mar. 1 Mdse. 2/10, n/30 72 36000 Mar. 10 Cash 2/10 

Apr. 12 Mdse. 2/10, n/30 84 280 00 Apr. 21 Cash 2/10 

May 10 Mdse. 2/10, n/30 96 415 00 

June 15 Mdse. 2/20, n/30 112 390 00

52 360 00 64 280 00 The account of Mr. Forbes, a customer, shows a balance of $805. Since he paid the March and April invoices ($360 and $280) within the 10 days, he received the 2% discount on each amount, but his account was credited for the full amount. 

Trade and Cash Discounts

Trade discounts are deductions, according to the terms of the sale, from the list price of the goods sold. Trade discounts serve

principally as a means of regulating prices. For example, one firm may offer a certain article for $20.00, less discounts of 40% and 10%; another firm may offer the same brand of article for $20.00, less discounts of 30% and 20%. While the discounts total the same in each case, in the first instance, the net price is $10.80; in the second, the net price is $11.20; and the difference between the prices is $.40.

How to find the equivalent discount, or a single discount equal to a series of two or more discounts, is explained on page 131. 

List price is the price at which the goods are listed, before the discounts are deducted.

Net cost is the cost of the goods after the trade discounts are deducted. The cash discount is not deducted to find the net cost; however, the cash discount is deducted when the net cost for cash is computed.

Cash discount is a discount allowed on a bill, or invoice, of goods, for payment made at a stated time before the full period of credit has expired. Hence, the terms "2/10, n/30" mean: a 2% discount will be allowed if the bill is paid within 10 days, but no discount will be allowed if the full term of credit (30 days) is taken. The letter "n" stands for net (clear of all deductions). 

Cash discount, while it may be offered to encourage prompt payment, is in reality a financing charge, or a charge to the customer for using credit.

The rate of discount expresses the number of hundredths of the bill to be deducted. Thus, 2% equals Too, or .02.

It is a common practice, in listing goods for sale, to offer both a trade and a cash discount. For example, an article may be priced at $40.00 (list price), less 25% (trade discount), with the terms: 2/10, n/30 (cash discount).

To find the cash discount, when the amount of the bill and the rate of discount are given.

Example. The total of a sale amounts to $382.60. Find the cash discount and the net amount of the bill of goods, sold on the following terms: 2/10, n/30. $382.60 X .02 (2%) = $7.65, discount $382.60 $7.65 $374.95, net cost for cash

PERCENTAGE 

131 The rate, expressed as hundredths, times the amount of the bill equals the cash discount. The amount of the bill minus the discount equals the net amount, or the net cost. Exercise 83 Find the cash discount and the net amount of the following: 

1. Amount of bill, $1,250.00; terms: 3/10, n/60. 

2. Amount of bill, $1,482.90; terms: 2/10, n/30. 

3. Amount of bill, $3,926.50; terms: 4/10, n/90. 

4. Amount of bill, $783.45; terms: 2/10, n/30. 

5. Amount of bill, $1,312.60; terms: 5/10, n/4 mo. 

6. Amount of bill, $288.75; terms: 3/10, n/60.

To find the net cost when the amount of the bill and the trade discounts are given. In a problem where there are two trade discounts, compute the first discount, and then compute the second discount on the remainder.

Example. Find the net cost of a chair listed at $60.00, less

Profit and Loss

Dealer B Which is Profit and loss are terms used to express the result of business transactions. Goods are sold at a profit when they are sold for more than their cost; and at a loss, when they are sold for less than their cost.

List cost is the invoice or catalog price of goods purchased. 

Net cost, or net invoice price, is the cost of goods purchased after all trade discounts have been deducted. 

Net cost for cash is the cost of goods purchased after all discounts have been deducted and the bill is paid in cash. 

Gross cost is the total cost after all charges-such as freight, cartage, storage, commissions, and insurance-have been added to the net cost.

Gross sales means the total of sales over a period of time at invoice prices.

Net sales means the amount of sales after all returned sales and allowances have been deducted from gross sales. 

Gross purchases means the total amount of goods purchased for trading purposes.

Net purchases means the result after all returned purchases and allowances have been deducted from gross purchases. 

Selling expense is the cost of marketing the goods. 

Gross profit is the amount resulting after the gross cost of goods sold has been deducted from the net sales. This total is the profit before any selling expense has been deducted from sales. 

Net profit is the result after the total selling expense has been deducted from the gross profit.

Selling the commodities of trade is one of our most extensive business activities. Each person who has something to sell hopes to make a profit. Very often goods are sold at a loss. 

Every merchant or dealer must know how to estimate profit and loss if he hopes to compete successfully with the keen competition. of the present day.

Net cost means the price after the trade discount has been deducted; sales price, or selling price, means the price at which the goods are sold, or are to be sold. Profit or loss is computed in per cent on the gross cost, on the net cost, or on the selling price. The base depends upon the result desired. 

Retail prices, in many cases, are prices determined without regard to the per cent of profit to be made. Different merchants may sell the same brand of article at widely different prices. 

For example, a certain kind of shoe, made to sell for $5.00 a pair, is sold by the manufacturer for $3.50 a pair. One retail merchant may sell this shoe for $5.50 a pair, another may sell it for $5.00, and a third may sell it for $4.50. Business conditions and competition definitely affect the pricing of articles for sale. 

To find the per cent of gross profit on the net cost, or on the gross cost, when the list price, the per cent of discount, the charges, and the selling price are given.

Examples.

To find the per cent of gross profit on the selling price, when the list price, the per cent of discount, and the charges are given. 

Example. A dealer bought a lamp for $12, less 25% discount.

He paid $1 for express charges, and sold the lamp for $15. 

What per cent of gross profit did he make on the selling price? $12 X .25 (25%) = $3, discount $12 

$3 = $9, net cost $9 + $1 $15 - $10 $5 ÷ $15 

$10, gross cost $5, gross profit .33 or 33% profit on selling price

It is quite generally the practice to figure the per cent of profit on the selling price, because the price at which an article is sold is always more or less definitely fixed-that is, there are not so many items to be considered in its determination, such as discount, freight, cartage, and buying charges.

When the per cent of profit on either the net cost or the gross cost is computed, the question of exact cost is always involved. 

There may be a number of articles of different kinds included in one shipment by freight. To apportion the freight charges and thus arrive at the exact gross cost of each article would not be an easy matter. Therefore, in many cases the net invoice price of the article is taken to find the gross profit; and the total charges are made a direct charge against the total gross profits, over a period of time, to find the net profit. 

In some instances the gross profit is very large as compared with the actual first cost of the article. Additional costs, such as advertising, selling, and servicing, make up a large part of the list cost of the article. An article may cost $40 to manufacture, and it may sell for $100. The per cent of gross profit on the first cost will be 150%, but when all the other costs are considered, the net profit may be comparatively small. When the $60 gross profit is figured on the selling price, the per cent of gross profit amounts to only 60% and appears to be a fair profit. 

For the reasons mentioned above, many dealers use the selling price when they wish to compute the per cent of profit. Exercise 87

To find the price at which the goods should be sold when the net cost, or the gross cost, and the desired per cent of profit are given. A merchant's business may be of such a nature that, according to his fixed costs of doing business-such as rent, salaries, and advertising-he finds it necessary to make a certain per cent of profit on the costs of all goods sold. Sometimes this per cent of profit is figured on the net cost, and sometimes on the gross cost; the base depends upon the nature of the business or the method followed.

When it is difficult to pro rate charges, such as freight and cartage, over a number of articles, then the per cent of profit is figured on the net cost, and the charges are deducted from the gross profit on sales to find the net profit. 

Example. A dealer bought an article for $35.00, less 20% and 5%, and sold it at a gross profit of 40% on the net cost.

$8.40 $26.60, the net cost $26.60 × .40 (40%) = $10.64, the gross profit $26.60 $10.64 $37.24, the selling price

It is customary to adjust prices to what would be considered usual prices. In this case the selling price, $37.24, would be changed to $37.25. Some merchants, however, retain the odd cents in marking their prices.

Cost of Doing Business 

Costs of doing business may be divided into fixed costs and operating costs. Fixed costs include rent, taxes, insurance, and interest on borrowed funds. Operating costs include such items as salaries, advertising, transportation, commissions, and materials. 

Certain costs of doing business may be grouped under buying expenses and selling expenses. Buying expenses include any costs connected with the purchase of goods, such as commissions, advertising, and handling; such expenses add to the cost of goods. Selling expenses include any costs connected with the selling of goods, such as salaries and commissions paid salesmen, advertising, traveling expenses, and handling charges.

It is generally not possible to estimate in advance the exact cost of doing business, since even approximate costs can be arrived at only when careful records are kept over a period of time. Such records can be used as a basis for estimating probable costs. 

To find the ratio, expressed in per cent, of the cost of doing business to the amount of gross sales. 

Example. A wholesale merchant's gross sales were $124,860.00 in one year, and in the same time his cost of doing business was $21,850.50. What per cent of his gross sales was his cost of doing business? $21,850.50 $124,860.00 = .17, or 171%

Therefore, the merchant's cost of doing business for one year was 17% of his gross sales.

Had this merchant figured on all sales a gross profit equal to 25%, his net profit would equal the difference between 25% and 171%, or 71%. This percentage would give him a basis for figuring his profits. The more he could decrease his cost of doing business without decreasing his sales, or the more he could

increase his sales without increasing his cost of doing business, the more, proportionately, his net profits would be increased. Exercise 90 1. The gross cost of an invoice of goods was $975. PRICING GOODS 

The success of any business depends on a fair margin of profit. 

In any merchandising business it must be understood that goods bought from the manufacturer or the jobber are bought in quantities at a wholesale price which is less than the price they are sold at retail, unless for some reason they are sold at a loss. 

Merchants find it advantageous to use a code in pricing goods offered for sale. For this practice, frequently a code is used in marking the cost price, while figures are used in marking the selling price. Some merchants number the article on the price tag and then keep a record of the cost prices of articles, according to number. Sometimes a code is used in marking both cost and selling prices.

Any word or phrase having ten different letters may be used as a code, for example:

MINERALOGY 

1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 0 

Repeater: X BUY FOR CASH 

1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 0 

Repeater: Z 

The repeater is used where two identical figures appear together in a price. If the first code, MINERALOGY, is used, the cost price $14.45 would be written ME.XR instead of ME.ER.

An article that cost $12.75 is marked to sell at $19.25. By the code MINERALOGY the article is marked 

MI.LR $19.25

By the code BUY FOR CASH, it is marked BU.CO 

$19.25

Both codes can be used at the same time. The code MIN- 

ERALOGY can be used for the cost price, and the code BUY 

FOR CASH can be used for the selling price: MI.LR

BS.UO

PRICING GOODS 

145 There is no limit to the selection and arrangement of codes in price marking. As a means of making it more difficult for the customer to determine the code marking, a word like mineralogy may be arranged backwards:

YGOLARENIM 

1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 0

COMMISSION AND BROKERAGE 

A commission merchant, or broker, is one who transacts business for a second party, called the principal.

COMMISSION AND BROKERAGE 

147 The charge for this service is called the commission, or brokerage. This commission, or brokerage, is usually a certain price per unit of weight or measure, or a certain percentage of the cost or selling price.

A guarantee is an additional charge made by a commission merchant to cover his personal liability when he guarantees the quality of the goods he buys or sells, or when he guarantees the collection of bills for goods sold by him on credit. 

A collection fee is a service charge made by the commission merchant when he makes the collections for goods sold on account. 

The shipper is the one shipping the goods to the commission merchant. The commission merchant in this instance is called the consignor. The goods shipped are called a shipment. See "Shipping by Freight," page 66.

The commission merchant, when he receives goods to be sold, is called the consignee. The goods to be sold are the consignment. 

The gross proceeds are the full amount, from the collection or sale, received by the commission merchant. 

The net proceeds are the amount remaining after all the charges have been paid.

An account sales is a statement, prepared by the commission merchant, showing the quantity, price, and amount of sale; all items of expense, including the commission charged; and the net proceeds. 

An account purchase is a statement, prepared by the commission merchant, showing the items purchased; the price; the charges, including the commission charged; and the total amount. 

To find the net proceeds of a sale on commission when the amount of the sale, the rate of commission, and all the charges are given.

Examples. 

1. A fruit buyer shipped to a commission merchant 1,500 baskets of grapes to be sold on commission. The grapes were sold for 90¢ a basket. The charges were as follows: freight, $92.50; inspection, 2¢ a basket; and commission, 3%. Find the net proceeds.

1,500 baskets at $.90

Find the amount of the sale. Find the commission. Add the commission with the other charges, and subtract the total charges from the amount of the sale. The difference is the net proceeds.

INTEREST 

Interest is the amount charged for the use of borrowed money, or the income for the use of money loaned or invested. Hence the bookkeeping terms are interest cost and interest earned. 

Next to the four fundamental processes, there is no part of arithmetic in more general use than that part which involves interest; consequently its importance is much emphasized. 

Example. Jordan loaned Harper $1,000, on his secured note, for 1 year. The interest at 6% amounted to $60. In other words, it cost Harper $60 for the use of $1,000 for 1 year, and Jordan received an income of $60 from the $1,000 that he loaned to Harper. Therefore, the interest cost to one party, the borrower, is the interest income to the other party, the lender. 

The principal is the amount of money borrowed, loaned, or invested. In the example given, $1,000 is the principal.

The rate is the number of per cent charged for the use of money. In the example above, 6% is the rate. 

The time is the period for which the money is borrowed (years, months, days). In the example, the time is 1 year.

There are three kinds of interest: ordinary, or simple, interest; accurate, or exact, interest; and compound interest. 

Ordinary, or simple, interest is figured on a basis of 360 days in a year (30 days to each month).

Accurate, or exact, interest is figured for the actual number of days, on a basis of 365 days in a year. (The actual days in each month are counted.)

Compound interest is interest both on the original principal and on the accrued interest from the time it falls due. 

Practically every state in the United States has an established interest rate, known as the legal rate. These rates vary in the different states from 5% to 8%. Charging more than the legal rate is called usury. In most of the states a penalty is imposed for usury.

The methods of figuring interest are commonly referred to as: the ordinary method, the exact method, and the bankers' method. 

The difference between these methods is in the way the time is reckoned.

The only reason for using the 360-day year when figuring interest is that its use simplifies the computation process. The year is considered as having 360 days, or 30 days for each of the 

12 months.

The 60-Day Method 

To find the amount of interest when the principal, the rate, and the time are given.

Examples. 

1. Find the interest on $1.00 for 60 days at 6%. 

When the rate of interest is 6%, the interest on $1.00 for 1 year is $.06. Therefore, the interest on $1.00 for 2 months, or 

60 days, is of $.06 (60 days is of 360 days, or of a year), or $.01, which is equal to interest at the rate of 1%.

INTEREST 

155 Hence, to find the interest on any principal for 60 days at 6%, find 1% of the principal, or point off two places. 

2. Find the interest on $750.00 for 60 days at 6%. The interest is $7.50. 

To find the interest on any principal at 6%: 

1. To find the interest for 60 days at 6%, point off two places. 

2. To find the interest for 6 days at 6%, point off three places. 

3. To find the interest for 5 days at 6%, take 

6 days. of the interest for 4. To find the interest for 4 days at 6%, take of the interest for 

6 days. 

5. To find the interest for 3 days at 6%, take of the interest for 

6 days. 

6. To find the interest for 2 days at 6%, take of the interest for 

6 days. 

7. To find the interest for 1 day at 6%, take of the interest for 

6 days.

If the interest has been found for 30 days, the interest for 3 days can be found by taking of the interest for 30 days. Likewise, the interest for 12 days is of the interest for 60 days. The interest for 5 days is of the interest for 30 days, or of the interest for 60 days. 2 In solving simple interest problems by the 60-day method, always use the most direct and simplest process.

Examples.

The result is $3.08.

USE OF ALIQUOT PARTS 

When interest is found by the 60-day method, 60 days is the basis, and the aliquot parts of 60 are used when the time is greater or less than 60 days.

SIMPLIFIED METHODS 

Method 1. To simplify the process, the amount of the principal and the number of days may be interchanged. 

Example. Find the interest on $150.00 for 158 days at 6%. $158.00 for 150 days at 6% $1.58, the interest for 60 days 

1.58, the interest for 60 days .79, the interest for 30 days $3.95, the interest for 150 days 

Interchange the $150.00 and the 158 days. Using the 150 as days instead of principal gives two 60-day periods and one 

30-day period. With 158 days as the principal, the interest is easily found to be $1.58 for 60 days, or $3.95 for 150 days.
