The 1-Day, or Product, Method 

To find the interest on any principal at 6% by the 1-day, or product, method. Point off three places—that is, remove the decimal point three places to the left; multiply by the number of days; and divide the product by 6.

Examples. 

1. Find the interest on $395.00 for 51 days at 6%. $.395, interest for 6 days at 6%, or for 1 day at 36% $.395 X 51 (days) $20.145, interest at 36% $20.1456 (6%) = $3.3575, or $3.36, interest at 6%

Removing the decimal point three places to the left gives the interest on the principal for 6 days at 6%, or for 1 day at 36%, the same as in the 60-day method. Multiplying by the number of days gives the interest at 36%. Dividing by 6 (6% = 1/ of 36%) gives the interest at 6%. 

2. Find the interest on $2,982.60 for 11 days at 6%. $2.98260 X 11 ÷ 6 $5.4681, or $5.47, interest 144 The 1-day, or product, method can be used to advantage when the time cannot be divided easily into aliquot parts of 60. 

This method also serves as a means of proving problems solved by some other method.

All problems in simple interest can, however, be worked by the 60-day method. This method is preferred to all others because it lends itself more readily to mental calculations, and thereby saves considerable time in computations. 

Finding the Time

To find the due date, or the interest period, a current calendar should be used. When the due date falls on a Sunday or on a holiday, the next business day is the due date, and this extra time is counted. In banking circles in some localities, Saturday is considered a holiday when the interest period is calculated. 

ORDINARY METHOD 

To find the time by the ordinary method. In the ordinary method of finding the time, a year is considered as having 12 months of 30 days each, or 360 days. 

Example. Find the time between March 18, 1933, and 

September 12, 1934. 

By compound subtraction, Year 1934 1933 1 Month Day 12 18 24 

Borrow 1 month. Then, 30 days (1 month) + 12 days = 

42 days; 42 days 18 days 24 days. Now, 9 months becomes 8 months; 8 months 3 months 5 months. And, 1934 1933 1 year. The result is: 1 year 5 months 24 days. 

---

INTEREST 

163 Exercise 103 By compound subtraction, find the time between: 

1. May 4, 1932, and June 17, 1934 

2. April 14, 1931, and March 12, 1934 

3. January 22, 1932, and July 15, 1934 

4. February 7, 1933, and December 16, 1934 

5. October 16, 1932, and May 11, 1934 

6. September 27, 1932, and January 15, 1934 

7. August 24, 1933, and February 18, 1934 

8. July 7, 1932, and March 19, 1934

EXACT METHOD 

To find the exact time in days. To find the exact time, the actual number of days in each month is counted. 

Example. Find the exact time from March 21 to July 16 of the current year. 

March: 10 days (remaining)

April: 30 

May: 31

June: 30 July: 16 

117 days

In this problem, the time is counted from March 21; there are 

10 days remaining in March. The 16th of July is counted.

To count the last date but not the first date is the usual practice. 

In some localities, however, when commercial paper is discounted, it is customary to count the first date and also the last date. 

Had this practice been followed in the above problem, the time would have been 118 days.

In the problems following, where the exact time is used, it is to be counted in the usual way. Exercise 104

4. January 12 and April 9

6. July 18 and October 21

BANKERS' METHOD 

To find the time by the bankers' method. By the bankers' method, the time is expressed in months and days, or in days only. Exact time is counted by the calendar. This method is used to find the time for short periods. 

Example. Find the time, by the bankers' method, from 

March 22 to July 28.

A banker, when figuring the discount on a note, would count the exact time as follows: March: 

9 days (remaining) April: 30 May: 31 June: 30 July: 28 

Discount period: 128 days

The time between the date of discount (March 9) and the due date (July 28) is 128 days.

In case the paper (note or draft) discounted falls due on a Sunday or a holiday, an extra day must be added to the discount period. 1 1 or 739 78, In a year there are 365 days; but when ordinary interest is figured, the 360-day year is used. This practice is followed to simplify the process. Hence, the exact year is, or, longer than the ordinary year; and the ordinary year is 85, or shorter than the exact year. For this reason, the anyone else who loans money and counts the exact time when reckoning his interest on a basis of 360 days in a year, receives a little more interest than he otherwise would.

$29.25, the bankers' interest 360 100 By the exact method, the exact time is counted and the year is considered as having 365 days. By the bankers' method, the exact time (plus holidays and Sundays) is counted, and the interest is figured by the ordinary method on a basis of 360 days to the year. The bankers' method produces the most interest. 

For 115 days, the exact interest is less than the ordinary interest. 1 73 $28.75 X $28.75 115 $1,500 X X 1 73 $.39 365 100 $.39 $28.36, the exact interest for 115 days $28.36, the exact interest for 115 days

Hence, for the same number of days, the exact method produces the least interest, and the bankers' method produces the most interest.

In the illustration used, the exact time is 2 days more than the ordinary time. This accounts for the small difference between the ordinary interest and the exact interest, and explains why the exact interest in this case is more than the ordinary interest.

The bankers' method is further illustrated below. 

Example. Find the bankers' interest on $1,800 at 6% from 

October 8 of the current year to February 4 of next year.

120 days (February 4 is on Sunday). Count the time from 

October 8 to see if 120 days is correct. $1,800 for 120 days at 6% $18, the interest for 60 days

COMPOUND INTEREST 

To find the amount of compound interest when the principal, the rate of interest, and the time are given.

Tables for time, ordinary interest, accurate interest, and compound interest are available at some stationery stores; many publishers also sell such tables. CREDIT 

A very large part of the world's business is conducted on credit. At various times municipal and state governments and the Federal Government borrow money on bonds or on short-

term credit instruments such as notes. Individuals and firms establish lines of credits to buy goods on account or to borrow money for various purposes.

The creditor, the one who loans money or accepts credit, should always be well informed on the following points: that the debtor is a good financial risk (Capacity), that he is possessed of a high degree of integrity (Character), and that he is financially responsible (Capital). 

There are a number of agencies that make a business of supplying credit information about the social, business, and financial standing of prospective debtors, or those who have applied for credit. Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., is the largest general credit agency. Credit agencies secure credit information concerning individuals and firms, and, on application, furnish it to their subscribers; such information can be secured from these agencies only by subscribers.

Credit ratings are given in different ways. Sometimes letters are used to show the standing-for example, triple A; double A; or A, B, C, or D rating. Sometimes numbers are used-for example, 1, 2, 3, or 4, signifying first-class, second-class, thirdclass, or fourth-class rating.

The one desiring to establish a credit standing is asked, by the agency concerned, to supply full information about his finances and to furnish references as to his responsibility. The agency also makes first-hand investigations. 

Negotiable Paper

The two principal means of establishing credit are through the charge account, or the book account, and through the written promise to pay.

The written promises to pay a certain sum of money at a definite future time, which are used so extensively in practice, are known as negotiable paper. 

Negotiable paper includes promissory notes, drafts, bonds, and other credit instruments.

CREDIT 173 

PROMISSORY NOTES 

The accompanying illustration is a promissory note. $1,000.00. 

Sixty days the order of Charles J. Hammond 

New York, No Yes January 11, after date___L_ 19. promise to pay to

One thousand and 00/100-----at the Merchants National Bank of New York. 

Value received

Due March 12, 19-- 

Howard Milton

DOLLARS 

Promissory Note.

Howard Milton is the maker of this note, and Charles J. 

Hammond is the payee. 

Pay to the order of 

Adam Kemper 

Charles J. Hammond 

Full Indorsement.

In case Charles J. Hammond wishes to transfer this note to another person, he can do so by indorsing it on the back, as shown in the accompanying illustration. This is a full indorsement; the note, when indorsed in this way and transferred, becomes the property of Adam Kemper. If Mr. 

Kemper wishes to transfer the note to another party, it will be necessary for him also to indorse the note. 

There are several kinds of indorsements, but the most common are: the full indorsement, as described above; and the blank indorsement, which consists of the payee's name only. When the blank indorsement is used, the paper becomes payable to bearer and does not have to be indorsed again for transfer to another party.

The person who indorses negotiable paper guarantees that it is a good contract; that he is the rightful owner; and that he will pay the amount of the paper when due, in case the maker fails to pay.

TIME DRAFTS 

This sample time draft is a three-party time draft. Charles E. 

Banks is the maker, Andrew J. Cook is the payee, and James R. 

Baker is the drawee. Mr. Baker is expected to accept this draft when it is presented to him by Andrew J. Cook, or someone 200.00.

DOLLARS 

Charles E. Banks acting for Mr. Cook. The drawee accepts the draft by writing across the face (front) of it: 

Accepted January 21, 19James R. Baker

After this draft is accepted, it is in effect the same as a 30-day promissory note. It has 30 days after January 21 to run, and it would be possible for Mr. Cook to have the note discounted at the bank where he does business. 

Drafts are used as a means of collecting and settling accounts. 

Where a draft is used as a means of collecting an account, it is usually made payable to the order of "Myself," signed by the maker, and then indorsed by him and transferred to the bank that will act as agent in collecting the draft. For this service, banks charge a small fee, called collection.

Bank Discount

Bank discount is interest charged by a bank, or banker, when negotiable paper is accepted, from the owner, for discount before maturity. The owner of the paper must indorse it over to the bank, as security for the money advanced by the bank. The bank may hold the note until it is due, or rediscount it at another bank. Federal Reserve banks rediscount notes and time drafts for banks that are members of the Federal Reserve Banking System.

The discount, or interest, is the bankers' charge for advancing payment on the paper.

The face value of a note or draft is the amount for which the instrument is drawn.

The difference between the amount that the banker will collect at maturity and the interest that is charged is the net proceeds. 

The due date, or date of maturity, is the date on which the paper becomes due and payable. 

When a promissory note becomes due, the bank collects from the maker; or, if the maker fails to pay, from the indorser, the one for whom the bank has discounted the paper.

When a time draft or a trade acceptance becomes due, the bank collects from the drawee; or, if the drawee fails to pay, from the payee (the indorser), the one for whom the bank has discounted the paper.

The discount period is the time between the date of discount and the due date.

Bank discount is computed in the same way as simple interest. 

Bank discount is figured for the time (actual number of days) between the discount date and the due date, and an extra day is counted when the due date falls on Sunday or on a holiday. 

The two classes of paper offered for discount are non-interestbearing and interest-bearing. 

To find the due date. Where the time is given in days (60 days, 90 days, and so on), count ahead from the date of issue of the paper the number of days given. For example, "Sixty days after November 1" means that the due date is December 31. 

November: 29 days (remaining)

December: 31 

60 days

Where the time is given in years or months, count ahead from the date of the paper the number of years or months given. For example, "Four months after March 22" means that the due date is July 22. 

March 22 to April 22: 1 month to May 22: 1 to June 22: 1 to July 22: 1 4 months To find the discount period. The difference in time between the date of discount and the due date is the discount period, or the period for which the discount (interest) is computed. 

Banks use tables for finding the time (discount period) and for computing the interest (bank discount).

INTEREST-BEARING NOTES 

To find the bank discount and the net proceeds of interestbearing notes. When an interest-bearing note is discounted, the interest, at the stated rate for the full time the note is given, is added to the face value. This result gives the amount of the note when the paper becomes due, and it is this amount on which the discount is figured, at the stated rate of discount, for the discount period. The net proceeds are found by deducting the discount from the amount of the note. $400.00. 

Detroit, Mich., Jan. 2, 19Four months after date. I promise to pay to the order of 

Elmer C. Jackson

DOLLARS 

Four hundred and 00/100 at the Commercial National Bank of Detroit 

Value received. with interest at 6%

No........ Due May 2, 19-- Arthur L. Downs Interest-Bearing Note.

CREDIT 

181 Before the bank would accept this note for discount, Mr. 

Jackson would have to indorse the note as shown in the accompanying indorsement illustration. 

Elmer C. Jackson 

Blank Indorsement.

Example. Find the net proceeds of the above note. It was discounted at the Commercial National Bank of Detroit on March 3 at 6%. $400.00 for 4 months (due May 2) at 6% $4.00, interest for 2 months (60 days) at 6% 

4.00, interest for 2 months (60 days) at 6% $8.00, interest for 4 months at 6% $400.00+ $8.00 

From March 3 to May 2 

$408.00 

$408.00 $4.08 $4.08 $408.00, amount at maturity 

60 days, discount period amount for 60 days at 6% discount for 60 days at 6% $403.92, net proceeds

To find the net proceeds on interest-bearing notes: (1) Find the due date. (2) Find the interest, at the given rate, for the full time of the note. (3) Add the interest to the face of the note to find the amount at maturity. (4) Find the discount, at the given rate of discount, on the amount for the term of discount. (5) Subtract the discount from the amount to find the net proceeds.

Settling with Creditors 

The use of money is considered to be worth at least the legal rate of interest, which is the rate that may be charged when interest is allowable and no rate of interest is mentioned.

CREDIT 

187 In the case of a past due account, the law will, as a rule, permit the creditor to charge interest, at the legal rate, on the overdue items. Also, when a non-interest-bearing note becomes due and is not paid, the holder may charge the maker interest, at the legal rate, for the time past due. On an interest-bearing note on which payments are made at intervals, interest is figured, at the rate specified, on each unpaid new balance for the time it remains unpaid. However, in such instances, a new note may be given for the amount unpaid, and the old note taken up. 

This practice is frequently followed.

PAYING A NOTE 

Since it sometimes happens that the maker of a note cannot pay the full amount when the note is due and payable, it becomes for him to pay a portion at a time until the full amount necessary is paid.

Notes are made payable either on demand after date or at a determinable future time, such as, 90 days after date, or 6 months from date. In the case of a non-interest-bearing note, interest may be charged for the time between the due date and the date of settlement, or for the time the note is past due. In the case of an interest-bearing note, interest is charged for the full time of the note and for the time the note is past due.

A note is fully discharged by paying the face of the note plus any unpaid interest.

After a note has run for 6 years during which no interest or no part of the note has been paid, the note is said to have become $2,000.00. 

Buffalo, N. Y., January 2, 1933 

Six months after date I the order of

Samuel Green promise to pay to 

-DOLLARS 

Two thousand and 00/100at the office of the Green Construction Company 

Value received.. with interest at 6% per annum

TAXES 

Everyone is affected more or less by taxes: some persons, because they have to pay taxes; others, because they enjoy the benefits resulting from taxes. Taxation makes it possible to carry on many public institutions and governmental activities, all of which are necessary for the common good. 

A tax is a sum charged, by an organized department of government, against certain individuals and against property owned by individuals or private organizations, for the purpose of raising money to defray the expenses of government or of operating public institutions.

The two kinds of taxes are direct and indirect. 

Direct taxes are charged against individuals and against property.

Indirect taxes are in the form of duties, customs, and excise taxes, which are explained in later sections of this text. 

Among the taxes levied are license fees, or franchise taxes, which are charged for the privilege of doing a certain thing, or

TAXES 

193 conducting a certain business. In some states, voters are required to pay a poll tax.

The most common form of direct tax is the property tax, which is the tax on privately owned property, real and personal, assessed at a certain value. The property tax is charged against this amount, according to the tax rate. Property tax which has been levied against real property (real estate) serves as a lien on that property until the tax is paid. 

The tax rate is the result obtained by dividing the amount of tax to be raised in a certain district by the total assessed valuation of taxable property in that district-for example, a town, village, or city.

Assessors are officers elected or appointed to make up the assessment roll for a certain district. The assessors must decide on the value of the property to be taxed. The assessed valuation of property is presumed to be either its market value (the amount the property would bring if sold) or a specified per cent of its market value-60%, 80%, and so on-depending on whether it is the plan of the local officials to assess property at its full value or at a percentage of its full or market value. 

Assessing property to be taxed presents many problems. 

The person whose property is assessed too high pays more than his just share of tax, and the person whose property is assessed too low does not pay his full share. 

The assessment roll contains listings of all property to be taxed, with the names of the individual owners. 

Collectors of taxes are officers elected or appointed to collect taxes from taxpayers.

Collectors' fees are fees allowed the collectors or charged by them for collecting taxes.

Tax Budget 

Each division of government-village, town, city, county, state, or national-makes up its own budget of expenses, for which taxes, in some form, are levied and collected. In the case of a city government, each department of the city government, through its department head or administrator, submits

to the chief executive, the tax department, or the board of estimate an itemized list of all probable expenses for the coming tax year. The final tax budget is made up from the totals submitted by each of the different departments. 

In a city government the most important departments are as follows: general government; public safety; health; highways; debt service; public schools; and many others, as the size of the city may demand.

To find the tax rate when the budget and the total valuation of property, as shown by the assessment roll, are given. When each department of a city government has submitted its budget and the total of the city budget has been estimated, the tax rate is found by dividing the total of the complete budget by the assessment roll total. 

Example. The total assessed valuation of the taxable property in a city is $150,580,290.00, and the amount of the budget is $3,689,217.10. Find the tax rate for this particular year. $3,689,217.10 ÷ $150,580,290.00 = .0245, or 2.45%, the tax rate 

For each $100.00 of assessed valuation, the tax is $2.45.

To find the tax rate, divide the total of the tax budget by the total of the assessed valuation, or the total of the assessment roll. Exercise 117 1. The assessed valuation of the taxable property in a certain village is $40,550,300.00 and the tax to be raised, as shown by the tax budget, is $1,034,032.65. Find: (a) the tax rate, in per cent; (b) the rate on each $100.00 of assessed valuation. 

2. The assessment roll showed the valuation of taxable property in a certain city to be $420,380,560.00, and the tax budget showed taxes to be collected amounting to $11,980,845.95. Find the tax rate per $100.00 of assessed valuation. 

3. A city tax budget calls for $57,726,896.08, and the assessment roll amounts to $2,380,490,560.00. Find the tax rate per $100.00 of assessed valuation. 

4. In a certain town the assessment roll totals $4,920,450.00, and the tax budget totals $108,741.95. Find the tax rate per $100.00 of assessed valuation.

TAXES 

195 The state, the county, the city, the town, or the village raises by direct tax the money necessary to run the different departments of its own government. The income tax is the only direct tax levied by the Federal Government. All other taxes levied by the National Government are in the form of indirect taxes.

The local tax department in any community adds with its own budget the amounts to be raised by that community for state and county purposes.

The state and the county taxes are apportioned among the different communities according to the assessed valuation of taxable property in that community. The state tax is apportioned among the counties, and the county tax plus the state tax is apportioned among the different tax districts of the county. It is the practice to put the amounts that a particular tax district is to raise for state and county purposes in the local budget before the local tax rate is determined. 

For a particular community, the county rate may be 3.2 mills ($.0032) per $1.00 of assessed valuation; the state tax may be 5.1 mills ($.0051); and the local tax may be $.0185. To find the tax rate, it would be necessary to add together the three rates ($.0032 + $.0051 + $.0185 $.0268). The result is $.0268 per $1.00, or $2.68 per $100.00 of assessed valuation.

TAXES

Income Tax 

The Federal Government and many of the states impose a tax on incomes which are in excess of certain amounts. 

The Federal income tax applies to all individuals living within the United States, whether citizens or not; to all citizens of the 

United States, whether residing at home or abroad; and to all corporations doing business in the United States. 

Individuals and corporations coming under the provisions of the income tax law must file with the United States Collector of Internal Revenue an income tax return (for the preceding year, January 1 to January 1) on or before March 15 of the current year.

An exception is made when fiscal year returns are made. 

Individuals and corporations who choose to make their returns for a fiscal year may do so on the 15th day of the third month after the close of their fiscal year. 

The tax may be paid in installments as follows: one fourth at the time of filing the return, and one fourth every three months until the tax is fully paid. Returns of non-resident aliens and foreign corporations are due on the 15th day of the sixth month following the close of the taxable year. 

On different dates a similar return must be filed with the State Tax Bureau in states having a state income tax. Not all states have income tax laws; moreover, in states having such laws the legislation varies considerably. Study the income tax law of your own state.

Total, or gross, income includes: salary earnings, income and gain from business or profession, profit from dealings in property, interest, rent income, royalties, profits from security investments, dividends, and other sources of gain. 

Total deductions include: taxes; interest paid; losses resulting from bad debts, fire, storm and various other sources; donations, not to exceed 15% of net income, to charity, church, and education; and other expenditures authorized by law. 

Net income is the difference between the total income and the allowable deductions.

The Federal income tax on incomes of individuals is classified as normal tax and surtax. Normal tax is the regular income tax on the amount of net income in excess of the allowable exemptions and credits. The rate is 4%. Surtax is an extra tax on incomes over a certain amount; the surtax is imposed in addition to the normal tax.

Income tax laws, like other laws, are revised from time to time. The latest complete information can be secured from the tax departments, both state and national. 

Federal Income Tax 

Returns. An income tax return must be filed by every citizen of the United States, whether residing at home or abroad, and by every person residing in the United States, though not a citizen thereof, whose income for the taxable year exceeds a specified amount.

Credits. The following credits are allowed by the Revenue Act of 1934. 

Credits for exemption. For the purpose of the normal tax and the surtax, the following credits are allowed against net income: (1) $2,500 for a married person who is living with husband or wife, or for the head of a family. (2) $1,000 for a single person or for a married person who is not living with husband or wife. (3) $400 for each person (other than husband or wife) who is dependent upon the taxpayer, if such dependent is under 18 years of age or is incapable of self-support because of physical or mental defects. 

Credits for dividends and interest. The following credits are allowed for the purpose of the normal tax, but not for the surtax: (1) All dividends received from a domestic corporation which is subject to Federal income tax. (2) All interest received on obligations of the United States which is included in gross income. (3) All interest received on obligations of a corporation organized under Acts of Congress (such as the Reconstruction Finance 

Corporation Act, the Federal Home Loan Act, and the Federal 

Farm Mortgage Act).

TAXES 

199 Credit for earned income. 

The 1934 Revenue Act allows an earned income credit for the purpose of the normal tax, but not for the surtax. The earned income credit is 10% of the earned net income, but not in excess of 10% of the net income. The credit is deducted from the net income before the normal tax is computed. 

Earned income means wages, salaries, professional fees, and other amounts received as pay for personal services actually rendered.

The earned net income on which the 10% credit is computed is the amount of earned income less the earned income deductions.

Earned income deductions are such deductions as are properly charged against earned income; for example, traveling expenses. 

Surtax. Surtax net income means the amount of the net income in excess of the personal exemption and the credit for dependents.

The rates and amounts of surtax under the Revenue Act of 1934, based on incomes subject to surtax, are shown in the table on page 199.

Corporation Income Tax 

All domestic corporations, including joint stock companies, associations, and insurance companies (except those exempt by reason of the nature of their business) created or organized in the United States or under the laws of the United States or any state thereof, are subject to the corporation income tax. 

The rate of income tax on corporations is 13% of the net income. Consolidated returns may be filed by railroad companies only; the rate on such returns is 15%.

United States Duties 

Duties, or customs, are taxes levied by the Federal Government upon commodities imported. 

The two kinds of duties are ad valorem and specific. A duty may be either ad valorem or specific. Both kinds are levied upon certain articles. 

Ad valorem duty means a duty levied "according to the value" of the commodity. This duty is a certain per cent either of the invoice price in the country from which the article is imported, or of the value as appraised by the United States 

Custom House officials.

Specific duty is a stated amount levied, according to quality, on certain articles as they are weighed or measured-for example, the duty is levied at so much per pound, bushel, yard, or other measure. A specific duty per ton means per long ton (2,240 pounds).

A tariff is a schedule of the duties on the different articles. 

Tare is a deduction made for the weight of the box, crate, or other packing material. Allowances are made for leakage and for breakage.

The two forms of entry for goods received are consumptive entry and warehouse entry. Consumptive entry is made for any article on which the duty is paid at once. Warehouse entry is made for any article on which the duty is not paid at once and which is, consequently, placed in a bonded warehouse.

A bonded warehouse is used for the storage of articles on which the duty has not been paid. The owner of such a warehouse is required to give to the Federal Government a bond that he will not permit the delivery of stored articles until the duty has been paid, unless the articles are subject to other regulations. 

The importer also gives a bond whereby he is obliged to pay the duty and to remove the articles within three years. Articles not removed within three years may be sold to pay the duty and the storage.

INSURANCE 

Insurance is a form of indemnity against loss. It results from an agreement whereby one party-an individual, a company, or an organization-for a consideration agrees to pay another party, up to a certain amount, for loss or injury which the first party has suffered from certain definite causes. 

The risk is the danger to which a party is exposed and which may result in a loss if the party is not protected by insurance. 

The insurer, usually a company, is the party who assumes the risk.

The insured is the party for whom the risk is assumed. 

The premium is the fee charged by the insurer for assuming the risk, or the consideration given by the insured for his insurance contract.

The insurance policy is the contract or agreement between the two parties.

The term of an insurance policy is always definite-that is, it covers one year or a specified series of years. 

There are many kinds of insurance, including fire, life, health, accident, automobile, liability, fidelity, and others, but all are based on the same fundamental principle. Fire insurance and life insurance are the two kinds of insurance most frequently taken. They are discussed in the following text. 

Fire Insurance 

Fire insurance is a contract whereby the insurer agrees to pay the insured for loss caused directly by fire, and for loss caused by water or chemicals used in extinguishing the fire. 

Uniform, or standard, policies are those adopted for use generally by insurance companies operating within a certain district.

INSURANCE 

209 The term floating policy, as sometimes used, applies to policies on property moved about. 

A blanket policy covers two or more things by one amount of insurance—for example, building and equipment under a single policy of $25,000.

A valued policy names a definite value to be covered rather than the cash value at the time the loss occurs. 

An open policy does not state the amount of insurance. This policy is used to insure stocks of commodities which are being continually added to or taken from, such as goods stored in a warehouse.

The co-insurance clause, which provides that the insured shall carry insurance on his property equal to a certain per cent of its value, is one of the most important policy clauses. 

The 80% co-insurance clause, which is the one most frequently used, provides that the insured shall carry insurance on his property equal to 80% of its value. 

Actual application of the 80% co-insurance clause. If the value of the property covered is $7,500, the insured should carry at least $6,000 insurance, which is 80% of the value. 

If he carries only $5,000 insurance, then he is entitled to collect only 5000 of the loss, he himself bearing 1000 of the loss. If the loss is $3,600, he can collect only $3,000 ($3,600 × 8000 $3,000). But if he carries $6,000 insurance, which is 80% of the value, then he is entitled to collect 8000 of the loss, or all of it up to the amount of the policy. 6000 Fire insurance policies contain many clauses which should be read by the insured. The following are a few of the more important.

The cancellation clause provides that either party, insurer or insured, may cancel the policy by giving notice. The insurer must give at least 5 days' notice and refund, pro rata, the unearned premium. 

The vacancy clause specifies the conditions under which the property insured may be left unoccupied. This clause varies according to locality.

The alienation clause provides that, in case of a change of ownership, the insurer shall be notified and the policy transferred to the name of the new owner; otherwise the policy becomes void. 

Insurance companies may, if they so desire, replace or rebuild damaged property rather than make a cash settlement. 

One company taking a very large risk may re-insure, and thereby distribute this risk among other companies. When two or more companies insure a property, in the event of loss each company pays its pro-rata share of the loss. 

When furniture or other articles are insured, the insurer should keep a complete inventory of the different articles insured. 

To find the premium to be paid on a fire insurance policy. 

Example. A dwelling house was insured for $15,000.00, with the Standard Fire Insurance Company, at a premium of $.50 per $100.00 for 3 years. Find the premium. 15000 100 X $.50 $75.00, premium paid for 3 years

It must be understood that insurance companies employ adjustors to investigate losses and to appraise the amount of damage or loss that the insurer will pay. If the insured is not willing to accept from the insurance company the amount offered, he can contest his case in court. Life Insurance 

Life insurance is an agreement by which the insurer, for a consideration called the premium, agrees to pay a stated sum at the death of the insured or at some specified future time. 

The premium is the consideration paid for the insurance. 

For the premium paid, the insured receives a policy contract from the insurer (an insurance company). The consideration is usually paid in advance, annually or semiannually. 

The beneficiary is the person, named in the policy, to whom the insurance is payable upon the death of the insured.

The three principal kinds of life insurance policies are: ordinary life; limited-payment life; and endowment. 

Under the ordinary life policy, the premium is paid annually, in advance, during the life of the insured, and the policy is payable at his death.

Under a limited-payment life policy, the premium is paid up during a term of years. At the end of this period the policy is paid in full. The insured may, at this time, accept the cash value of the policy in settlement, or he may allow the policy to continue in force as paid-up insurance. The insured receives interest on paid-up insurance. The interest is allowed to accumulate until the policy is paid. The policy is always payable at death, whether the term has expired or not.

Under an endowment policy, the premium is paid, in advance, for a definite term of 10, 15, 20, or 25 years. At the expiration of the term the policy is payable in full to the insured. If the insured dies before the term is up, the face of the policy, plus any dividends credited to the policy, is payable at once. 

A term policy is a policy issued generally for financial protection during a fixed term. This policy is payable only in case of the death of the insured during the term, at the expiration of which the insurance ceases or may be converted into some other form of policy. 

There are many different kinds of investment, life, and income policies issued by the different companies. 

Insurance contracts provide for certain privileges on the part of the insured. (1) He may borrow money from the insurers up to the loan value of his policy, as stated therein. (2) He may surrender his policy to the insurer and accept the cash surrender value, thereby terminating the policy. (3) He may discontinue paying the premiums, and accept the paid-up insurance or an extension of time according to the stipulations in the policy.

To find semiannual rates and quarterly rates. For semiannual rates, add 4% to the annual rate and divide by 2; for quarterly rates, add 6% to the annual rate and divide by 4. 

Example. If the annual rate is $51.32, find: (a) the semiannual rate; (b) the quarterly rate. $51.32 X .04 (4%) $51.32 $2.0528 $53.3728 ÷ 2 $2.0528 $53.3728 $26.6864, or $26.69, semiannual premium rate $3.0792 $51.32 X .06 (6%) $54.3992 $51.32 + $3.0792 $54.3992 ÷ 4 = $13.5998, or $13.60, quarterly premium rate

To find the total cost of carrying an insurance policy.

Examples.

$24.18 5 = $120.90, annual premium on $5,000.00 $120.90 X 15 $1,813.50, total cost for 15 years

Some insurance companies do not drop any fraction of one cent in adjusting semiannual and quarterly premiums. In that case the semiannual premium on this policy would be $12.58 per each $1,000.00.

Assignment 53 1. A man, at the age of 27, took out an ordinary life policy for $5,000.00. He died at the age of 72. The dividends on this policy amounted to $1,368.20. At the end of the 20 years the cash surrender value of the policy was $2,077.02. (a) How much did the policy cost him? (b) How much more, or less, than he paid in did he receive? (c) How much would his premiums have amounted to at 4% compound interest for the 20 years? 

To find the compound interest, multiply the annual premium by the amount of $1.00 placed annually at compound interest for 20 years. See interest table, page 170.

He died after making 20 annual premium payments. The cumulated dividends on the policy up to the time of his death amounted to $116.27 per $1,000.00. How much more than he had paid in premiums did his heirs receive from the policy? 

4. A man, aged 25, took out a 10-year endowment policy for $2,000.00.

He paid his premiums annually. When the policy matured, he received $2,386.60. During the 10 years he was carrying the policy, he deposited in his savings bank each year an amount equal to the annual premium. 

At 4% interest, compounded annually for 10 years, how did his savings account compare with his life insurance return? (See table of premium rates page 215; also, interest table, page 170.)

The cents less than $1.00 are dropped when savings bank interest is computed. However, in this problem the cents are not dropped, as the problem serves only to make an approximate comparison between policy returns and bank returns. 

It must be understood that the policy affords financial protection to a greater extent than does the savings account.

CORPORATION STOCKS 

Corporations are either public or private. Public corporations are formed for governmental purposes, and include those of villages, towns, and cities. Private corporations are organizations of individuals and are authorized by law for many different purposes. 

The two kinds of private corporations are stock and non-stock. 

A stock corporation is organized for business purposes, and operates under a charter, granted by the state, which authorizes the corporation to transact business, within prescribed limits, in the same way that an individual might do so. A non-stock corporation is organized for religious, educational, charitable, or social purposes, and also holds a charter, from the state, which outlines the general purposes and privileges of the organization. 

The certificate of incorporation is a formal document issued by the Secretary of State, under the authority of the corporation laws of the state or of special legislation. This certificate, when it is filed with the county clerk of the county where the corporation has its headquarters, authorizes the corporation to start and carry on the business for which it was incorporated. Laws vary in the different states. 

The capital stock of a corporation is the amount authorized to be paid into the corporation for use in conducting its business. 

The capital stock of a corporation is divided into a certain number of shares. Ownership in a corporation comes from acquiring shares, either by purchase or otherwise. 

Shares of stock owned in a corporation are represented by a stock certificate which shows the number of shares purchased. 

The illustration on page 219 is a typical certificate of stock. 

Stock has a par value or a no-par value, and a market value. 

The par value of a share is its face value, which is usually $100 per share. However, the par value may be any amount.

CORPORATION STOCKS 

219 The market value of stock is the price at which a share can be sold or purchased. The dividend that stock pays and the financial standing of the corporation largely determine the market value of the stock.

No-par stock is stock that has no par value. Such stock is offered to subscribers, or first purchasers, at a certain price.

After the first sale the value of this stock depends on the market price. In other words, no-par stock has a market value only. SHARE 00000 00000

SPECIMEN 

SHARE 

AMERICAN TELEPTING & TELEGRAPH COU Certificate of Stock. 

The stock certificate represents the number of shares, but no face, or par value, is given.

A dividend is a sum of money paid by a corporation, out of its earnings, to its stockholders. Dividends are allotted according to the number of shares of stock owned by each stockholder. 

Dividends may be paid out of surplus. The directors of a corporation, if they see fit, may pass any particular dividend. 

Poor business, with subsequent small earnings, would be a good reason for passing a dividend.

Common stock is the stock which is given no preference in the distribution of profits or the division of assets. Whatever

profits are left after the interest on bonds has been paid and the dividends on preferred stock have been allotted, are distributed as dividends on common stock.

Preferred stock is stock which carries with it an agreement that a stated dividend, or percentage of the net profits, will be paid on that stock before any dividend is paid on common stock. 

Preferred stock is also given preference in the division and distribution of assets.

Cumulative preferred stock carries a guarantee that, in case regular dividends, for some reason, are not paid when they should be, they will be allowed to cumulate and will later be paid before any dividends are paid on common stock. All preferred stock is considered to be cumulative, unless the certificate of incorporation states otherwise. 

A stock broker is one who buys and sells stocks for other people. He acts as agent, and the transaction risk is assumed by the one for whom he is acting. His fee is called brokerage. 

The brokerage rate charged for his services is a certain price per share or a certain per cent of the par value of the stock. Most brokers have a minimum fee, or commission, which they charge on any single transaction. Where only a small number of shares are bought or sold, some brokers charge a rate slightly higher than the regular rate. The odd lot rate for buying or for selling less than 100 shares is either or of 1% additional, the percentage depending upon whether the sale of the stock is active or inactive-that is, % is charged on fast selling stock, and 1%, on slow selling stock. This fee is added to the price of the stock (48+ or 1). 

On the Stock Exchange in New York City the fees are as shown on page 221. 

Buying a membership seat on a stock exchange means buying a right to trade in stock on the floor of the exchange. 

Listed stocks are stocks which may be bought and sold on the floor of the exchange.

Unlisted stocks are stocks which may be dealt in by anyone else, but which cannot be bought and sold on the floor of the exchange. Such stocks are usually marketed by brokerage

CORPORATION STOCKS 

221 houses, banks or private bankers, and investment firms, and are said to be sold over the counter.

BROKERAGE RATES ON NEW YORK STOCK EXCHANGE For Stock Selling At 

$.50 and under $ 1.00. $ 1.00 and under $ 10.00. $ 10.00 and under $ 25.00. $ 25.00 and under $ 50.00. $50.00 and under $ 75.00. $75.00 and under $100.00. $100.00 and under $200.00. $200.00 and over.. 

Fee per 100 Shares $ 3.00 7.50 12.50 15.00 17.50 20.00 25.00 $30.00 per 100 shares, plus $5.00 additional per 100 shares for each $50.00, or fraction thereof, beginning with $250.00

A trader in stock is anyone who buys and sells stock for his own account and at his own risk. A bull is a trader who operates for a rise in prices. A bear is a trader who operates for a decline in prices.

A trader who buys stock to hold for a rise in prices is said to be long in stock. A trader who sells stock that he does not ownbut which in reality he borrows from his broker for the purpose of selling—expecting to buy the stock at a lower price and return it to his broker, is said to be selling short. 

Buying stock on margin is a common practice. In such a transaction, the trader furnishes only a part, or margin, of the money necessary to finance the transaction, and the broker completes the financing of the transaction by loaning the remainder of the purchase price; or, the trader may borrow the remainder from a bank. In either case, the stock purchased is deposited with the lender, as security for the money borrowed.

The loan which a bank makes to a broker in a stock buying transaction is known as a call loan—that is, the banker may, at any time and without notice, call for payment of the loan. The rate of interest charged for a call loan may be high or low, according to the money market.

At the present time marginal requirements are fixed by the Federal Reserve Board, appointed by the President of the

United States and given full power to act under a Federal law, passed by Congress, known as the "Securities Exchange Act of 1934." The marginal requirements at the time of this writing are from 25% to 45% of the market value of the stock; the percentage depends on the marketability of the stock and its price fluctuation over a period of time.

The Federal tax levied on all sales of stock on which a transfer of ownership takes place is as follows: on stock selling below $20 per share, the tax is 4¢ per share on a par value of $100; on stock selling above $20 per share, the tax is 5¢ on each $100 of par value.

In New York State there is an additional state tax levied on all sales of stock on which a transfer of ownership takes place. 

This tax is levied as follows: on all stock selling under $20 per share, the tax is 3¢ per share on a par value of $100; on stock selling above $20 per share, the tax is 4¢ on each $100 of par value. 

Both of these taxes must be paid by the seller. For example, stock sold on the New York Exchange, which results in a transfer of ownership, must bear a tax of $7 (or $9) per one hundred shares, par value $100.

If the par value is less than $100, the tax is proportionately less, according to the number of shares. Thus, if the par value is $50, the Federal tax per share would be 2¢ or 2, according to the market price of the stock.

If the selling price is less than $20, no-par stock is taxed 4¢ a share; if the price is $20 or more per share, the rate is 5¢ instead of 4¢. 

The original issue of the capital stock of a corporation is subject to a Federal tax of 10¢ on each $100 par value; on stock of no-par value, the tax is 10¢ per share. Or, if the actual value of the issue of stock is in excess of $100, then the tax is levied at the rate of 10¢ per $100; or, if less than $100, at 2¢ on each $20 of actual value, or fraction thereof.

Sales means the number of shares bought and sold on this particular day; Open, the price the first sale brought; High, the highest price at which a sale was made during the day; Low, the lowest price at which a sale was made during the day; Close, the last price at which a sale was made that day; Net Change, the difference between this day's last price and yesterday's last price.

Exchange prices are quoted in dollars and cents per share. 

Thus, "120" means $120.25 per share, par value $100 or no-par value. The fraction of a dollar used in stock quotations is always or some multiple of (1, 1, 1, 1, and so on). To find the cost of stock when the price per share, the number of shares, and the brokerage charge are given.

BONDS 

A bond is a secured credit instrument, under seal, which carries with it a promise to pay a specific sum of money within a fixed time, and to pay interest thereon at a fixed rate at regular intervals. 

The two general classifications of bonds are municipal (Federal, state, county, city) and corporation bonds.

Bonds are designated by many different names. The quota- tions published in newspapers give an idea of the many different bond designations. Examples are: Amalgamated Sugar Co. 

1st 7s, 1945; U. S. Treasury 4s, 1944-54. While serving to identify the bond, each designation tells the issuer of the bond, the interest rate, and the date on which the bond is payable. 

The interest rate is indicated by the figure before the "s"; thus, "4s" indicates 4% interest.

The two principal forms of bonds are registered bonds and coupon bonds. Coupon bonds are more commonly used.

Registered bonds are recorded on the records of the corporation or government unit issuing the bonds. This record shows the name and address of the owner, together with such other information as is necessary for mailing the interest check or paying the principal, when due, to the right party.

Any bond may be registered as to principal. Registration protects the owner against payment of the bond to someone other than the rightful owner. Coupon and registered bonds are generally interchangeable. 

Coupon bonds are bonds to which interest coupons are attached. 

When an interest coupon becomes due, it is detached by the owner of the bond and cashed, like a check, at the office of the issuing organization or at a bank.

BONDS 

231 Bonds are bought and sold through agents and brokers in much the same way as stocks are purchased and sold. Some- 100

Coupon Bond. times banking houses or brokerage firms take over whole issues of bonds and sell them direct to their customers.

Bonds issued by industrial corporations are usually secured by mortages on the property of the corporation. It is a common

practice to borrow money on a note secured by a mortgage on property, either personal or real.

The rate of commission, or the brokerage fee, charged by brokers for buying and selling bonds for their customers is usually 12 (%) or 25¢ (1%) on each $100 of par value of the bond. Most brokers set a minimum charge. For many brokers this charge is 25¢ on each $100 of par value; minimum, $2. The rate on the New York Exchange is 1%.

Bonds are made out for different amounts: some are as low as $10, while bonds for $25, $50, $100, $500, and $1,000 are common. 

Most large issues are in denominations of $1,000, but $10,000 bonds are not uncommon.

Bonds bear interest at a definite rate; the interest is payable at fixed intervals, usually annually or semiannually. 

Bonds have a par value and a market value. The par value is the face value of the bond, or the amount for which it is made out. The market value is the amount for which it sells. These values are rarely the same. (See quotations in a current newspaper.) The kind of security back of a bond and the rate of interest it bears help to influence its market value. 

Bonds that sell for less than their par value are said to sell at a discount; bonds that sell for more than their par value, at a premium.

The Federal tax on bonds issued by corporations is 10¢ on each $100 of face value. The Federal sales tax on bonds issued by corporations is 40¢ on each $1,000 of face value or fraction thereof. Tax rates are subject to frequent changes. United 

States Government and other municipal bonds are exempt from tax.

When bonds become due, they are payable at their face value. 

Sometimes, when the issuing company reserves the right to call in its bonds before they are fully due, the bonds will be redeemed at a premium.

Bond Owners and Stockholders 

A bond owner is a creditor of the municipality or corporation issuing the bond, and has no voice in the management of either.

BONDS 

233 The interest on a bond is fixed, and bonds are purchased usually as an investment.

Stockholders have a voice in the management to the extent, at least, of voting for the directors. Each holder of voting stock has as many votes at stockholders' meetings as he owns shares of stock. Some stock does not carry with it the right to vote. 

Stock is not payable at any time, but continues as a source of ownership and income so long as the corporation functions. 

Dividends on stocks depend on the earnings of the corporation and are declared by the directors. 

Bond Quotations

Bond quotations are given by daily papers and by financial publications in about the same way that stock quotations are listed.

PARTNERSHIP SETTLEMENTS 

A partnership results from an agreement whereby two or more individuals are to conduct some business, and to share in the profits or to bear the losses resulting therefrom.

The profits may be shared equally, in proportion to the amount invested by each partner, or in any other way agreed upon by the partners. Several arrangements are in common use: (a) 

Each partner may draw a salary. (b) Interest may be allowed at a definite rate on each partner's investment, after which the profits may be divided equally. (c) The profits may be divided pro rata, according to the investments.

PARTNERSHIP SETTLEMENTS 

237 One partner may give full time to the business; another, little or no time. There are no restrictions as to the kind of agreement partners may make so long as the arrangement does not violate some legal regulation.

The partnership's, or firm's, present worth, or proprietorship, is the amount its assets are in excess of its liabilities at a particular time. The present worth is also the sum of the net investment and the net gain, or the difference between the net investment and the net loss. However, if the loss is in excess of the net investment, then the result will be the net insolvency. 

The partner's present worth, or proprietorship, is the sum of his net investment and his share of the net gain, or the difference between his net investment and his share of the net loss at any time, unless his share of the net loss is so great that he becomes insolvent.

The assets of a partnership include all forms of property belonging to the firm.

The liabilities of a partnership consist of the firm's debts or obligations to others.

The problems in partnership settlements deal with methods of finding each partner's share of the profit or loss, and each partner's net worth, and computing the proportionate division of assets in case of dissolution.

To find each partner's share of the profit, according to the agreement entered into by the partners. 

Example. Judson and Moore become partners in a furniture business. Judson invests $12,000, and Moore invests $8,000. 

Interest at 6% is to be allowed on each partner's investment, after which the gains or losses are to be shared equally. At the end of the first year the net profit is $3,480. Find each partner's present worth. $12,000 at 6% for 1 yr. $8,000 at 6% for 1 yr. 

$720, interest on Judson's investment $480, interest on Moore's investment $3,480 ($720 + $480) = $2,280, remaining profit $2,280 ÷ 2 (2 partners) = $1,140, each partner's share $12,000 + $720 + $1,140 $13,860, Judson's present worth $8,000+ $480 +$1,140 = $9,620, Moore's present worth

To find the net profit when each partner draws a salary, the amount of the salaries is deducted from the gross profit. As a rule each partner draws his salary weekly, bi-weekly, or monthly, and it is paid from the profits or earnings of the business. Exercise 135 1. Clark and Morrow invested $6,000 each in a hardware business. 

The agreement was that each partner should give full time to the business, and share the profits or losses equally. The net gain for the first year was $2,130. Find each partner's present worth. 

2. Howard and Martin invested $7,500 each in the grocery business. 

Each partner drew as salary $25 a week ($1,300 per year). The gross profit for the year, over and above the partners' salaries, was $7,890, and the expenses other than the partners' salaries amounted to $2,350. 

Find each partner's present worth at the end of the first year. 

3. Gates and Low invested in a tract of land as a speculation. Gates invested $30,000, and Low invested $20,000. They agreed to share the profits or losses in proportion to their respective investments. Within a year the tract was sold for $70,000. The expenses of handling the property amounted to $1,440. Find each partner's share of the net profit.

Total investment equals $30,000+ $20,000, or $50,000. 

Gates' share equals net profit X (30,000 ÷ 50,000). Low's share equals net profit (20,000 ÷ 50,000). 

4. Three partners invested $10,000 each in an oil business. Each partner drew a salary of $150 per month; these salaries were taken out of the gross profits before any division was made. The other expenses amounted to $3,120. The net profits were divided equally. At the end of the first year the firm's assets amounted to $48,960, and its liabilities amounted to $4,200. Find each partner's share of the net profit. 

Total investment equals $30,000. Gross profit equals $48,960 minus ($30,000+ $4,200). Gross profit minus salaries and expenses equals net profit. 

5. A invested $10,000 in a wholesale automobile accessories business, B invested $15,000, and C invested $25,000. The net gain, according to their agreement, was to be shared in proportion to their investments. 

Each partner drew his salary, of $200 per month, on the last business day of each month. The net gain for the year, after all salaries and operating costs had been deducted from the gross gain, was $15,525. Find each partner's share of the net gain.

GRAPHS 

239 6. E, F, and G are partners in conducting a food market. E invested $5,000, F invested $7,500, and G invested $12,500. Their agreement provides that each partner shall receive 6% interest on his investmentwhich interest shall be paid from the net profit-after which the remaining net earning is to be shared equally. The income and expenses statement at the end of the year showed a net profit of $8,310. 

Find each partner's share of the net profit, including the interest on his investment.

Each partner's share equals the sum of his portion of the net profit after the interest on the investments has been deducted, plus the interest on his investment. GRAPHS Graph means, literally, to write. As the term is used here, it designates a means of exhibiting, in a related pictorial way, certain statistics or other facts from which conclusions, in a comparative way, can be drawn. 

Bar Graph A 

Bar Graph A represents a retail merchant's sales for twelve years, 1923 to 1934, inclusive. 100,000, 90,000 80,000 70,000 60,000 50,000 40,000 30,000 20,000 10,000 26 

Bar Graph A. Retail Merchant's Sales for 12 Years.

From the zero point the two series of units-time, in years; and amounts, in dollars-are arranged in progression. The amounts progress upward along the vertical line: $10,000, $20,000, $30,000, and so on, up to $100,000, inclusive. The time progresses to the right along the horizontal line: 1923, 1924, and so on, up to and including 1934. 

The time key is one year. The amount key is each horizontal section representing $10,000. 

Questions on Graph A 

1. What are the two units of measure? 

2. How much were the sales the first year? 

3. How much were the sales the fifth year? 

4. How much were the sales the last year? 

5. How much more were the sales the fifth year than the first year? 

6. How much were the sales each year during the last five years recorded? 

7. How much did the sales increase the twelfth year as compared with the first year? 

8. How did the sales for 1928 compare with those for 1934? 

9. What was the per cent of increase in the sales for 1929 over those for 1923? 

10. Why is it necessary to have a graph key?

PART II DRILLS AND REVIEW ASSIGNMENTS

FUNDAMENTAL OPERATIONS

Review Assignment 7 

1. The operating expenses of a bus line for one year were $28,800. 

These expenses were apportioned as follows: gas and oil, .24; repairs, .14; replacements, .22; franchise tax, .05; wages of employees, .35. What amount was apportioned to each of the expense items? Prove your results. 

2. D. H. Howard, failing in business, owed R. M. Grant $3,865.00.

Settlement was made with the creditors, and Mr. Grant received $2,512.25 as his share in settlement of his claim. What decimal part of the whole claim was paid? 

3. There was 1,280 pupils registered in a certain school. On a particular day 64 pupils were absent. What decimal part of the pupils enrolled were absent on this day? 

4. A man divided his investments as follows: .35 in bonds, .25 in first mortgage loans, .40 in municipal and corporation stocks. If his investments totaled $150,000, what amount did he have in each? 

5. The total cost of operating a business for one year amounted to $24,500. The expenses were distributed as follows: heat, light, and power, 8.2 hundredths; supplies, 15.5 hundredths; wages and salaries of employees, 35.8 hundredths; rent, 24.5 hundredths; interest on borrowed money, 7.5 hundredths. The balance was consumed by incidental expenses. What amount was charged to each item of expense?

Review Assignment 13 1. Find the total net invoice price of the following. The discounts are 10% and 2%. 

5 pieces Serge: 542, 53¹, 553, 54¹, 512. 

5 pieces Broadcloth: 483, 472, 49¹, 482, 49³. 

5 pieces Cheviot: 52¹, 51³, 542, 56¹, 55². at $2.80 a yard at $4.60 a yard at $3.70 a yard

The small superior numbers in this problem indicate fractions of a yard. Thus, 542 means 54 yd. in that piece; and 4 is the denominator of the fractions understood in 53¹ (531) and 55³ (552).

Review Assignment 16 

1. A fruit dealer paid an agent 5% for buying fruit for him on the market. The agent bought 40 crates of oranges at $4 per crate. Each crate contained 18 dozen. Freight and cartage amounted to 60¢ per crate. The dealer sold these oranges at 40¢ per dozen. (a) How much did he make on the 40 crates of oranges? (b) What per cent of gross profit did he make on the gross cost? (Net cost plus commission plus freight charges equals gross cost.) 

2. A dealer bought chairs for $36.00 a dozen, net. The freight on a 4-dozen lot was $12.80. He desires to make a gross profit of about 

50% on the gross cost. For how much each must he sell these chairs? 

Dealers usually mark their prices in multiples of 5 or 10¢. 

For example, $3.98 would be $4.00, or possibly $3.95 if competition were keen. In the case of larger amounts, the prices are usually marked in even dollars, or sometimes in variations of 

25¢ or 50¢; for example, $26.00, $26.50, $26.25, or $26.75.

The kind of article, the volume of business, business conditions, and competition-all of these factors influence the pricing of articles offered for sale. 

3. An article that cost $16 net was sold at a gross profit of $8. If the cost of doing business was 25% of the sales, what per cent of net profit was made on the selling price of this article? (Net cost plus gross profit equals selling price.) 

4. After all discounts were deducted, the net cost of an article was $48.00. The buying expenses amounted to 5% of the net cost. This article was sold for $63.00. (a) How much was the gross profit? (b) 

What per cent of the gross cost was the gross profit? (c) How much was the net profit if the selling expenses were $8.50? 

5. A dealer bought 100-piece sets of dishes for $90 per set, net. The cost of freight and cartage amounted to $2 per set. He sold the dishes for $138 per set. (a) What per cent of gross profit did he make on the gross cost? (b) What per cent of gross profit did he make on the selling price?

Review Assignment 20 

1. A man gave a demand note for $1,650 on March 4 with interest at 6%. He paid the note in full on August 21. How much did he pay? Count the exact number of days from March 4 to August 21, inclusive, of the current year. 

Demand notes usually bear interest for the exact number of days, and the interest is figured by the ordinary method. 

2. Find the exact interest on $2,800 at 6% from May 16 to December 

2 of the current year.

3. Find the compound interest on $3,000 for 6 years at 5%. Use the compound interest table on page 169. 

4. Find the amount of $100 placed annually for 12 years at 4% compound interest. Use the compound interest table on page 170. 

5. A teacher deposited $500 in a savings bank. The bank paid 4% compound interest and, every 6 months, added the interest to the balance on deposit. How much did the teacher have to his credit in the bank at the end of 5 years? 

Use the table on page 169. To find the amount, take one half the rate (2%) and twice the time (10 years). 

6. Find the interest at 6% on $1,540 for 1 year, 3 months, and 27 days. Use the ordinary method.

Unless exact interest is specified, the general practice is to figure interest by the ordinary method, whether exact time or ordinary time is counted.

1. Find the total dividend on the following stock:

Shares Stock

Rate of Dividend

Beech-Nut Packing 

$100 3% 100

United Fruit $100 2% 2. Find the market value, based on a 6% interest rate, of stock paying dividends as follows. Since fractions in the quotations on stock

BONDS 

281 exchanges are given in halves, fourths, or eighths, list your answers at the nearest fractions. (a) Annual dividend is $4.50 per share. (b) Annual dividend is $3.00 per share. (c) Semiannual dividend is 2% (par, $100.00). (d) Annual dividend is 4% (par, $100.00). (e) Semiannual dividend is $1.75 per share. 

On no-par stock, the dividend is a certain amount per share. 

On stock having a par value, the dividend is usually a certain per cent of the par value. However, the dividend on par value stock may also be a certain amount per share, as in the case of no-par stock.

To find the market value per share, divide the dividend on one share by .06, or 6%.

42, 1939, at 912; brokerage, 1%. Interest is payable semiannually on 

January 1 and July 1. Tax, $2.40. Find the proceeds from this sale. 

The accrued interest from July 1 to December 1 is added to the proceeds. 

5. Government bonds, bearing interest at 41%, sell for 1013; brokerage, 1%. What rate of interest on the investment do they yield?

Divide the interest on one bond ($4.50) by the full cost (101% +1%). The interest rate on the investment will be a little less than 4%. Carry your result out three places beyond the decimal point.

The method used in this problem is the method to use when the interest returns on two different investments are to be compared.

BONDS 

283 

Buying Stocks and Bonds on Margin 

Mr. W. D. Howard, a speculator on the stock market, makes a practice of buying stock on margin. He deposits with his broker a sum sufficient to protect the latter against loss resulting from probable market fluctuations. The broker then lends Mr. Howard the additional amount necessary to buy the stock, and holds the stock purchased as security for the money loaned in the transaction. The broker in turn borrows money on call loans from a bank, using the stock held by him as security for this loan.

For the purpose of preventing the excessive use of credit for the purchase or carrying of securities, the Federal Reserve Board prescribes rules and regulations with respect to the amount of credit that may be extended and maintained on any security, other than an exempted security.

The Securities Exchange Act, passed by Congress in 1934, gave the President of the United States the power to appoint a Securities and Exchange Commission whose duty it is to regulate the purchase, sale, and exchange of securities (stocks and bonds). Under this same Act the Federal Reserve Board has the power to prevent the excessive use of credit for the purchase and carrying of securities.

The Federal Reserve Board has established the following margin requirements (whichever is higher): (1) 55% of the current market price of the security; or (2) 100% of the lowest market price of the security during the preceding thirty-six calendar months, but not more than 75% of the current market price.

The Federal Reserve Board has the power to change these regulations and requirements at any time.

The margin requirements at the time of this writing are, thus, as follows: The Act lists them as 55%, 75%, and 100%. Brokers, however, ordinarily speak of this same margin as 45% and 25% margin. 

Example. Mr. W. D. Howard deposited $2,000.00 with his broker and directed him to buy 100 shares of U. S. Steel. The broker bought the stock, on the New York Exchange, at 482; brokerage, $15.00 per 

100 shares. A 5% interest rate was charged on the loan made to 

Mr. Howard, amounting to $2,890.00.

Thirty days later, as directed by Mr. Howard, the broker sold the stock at the prevailing market price, which was 543; brokerage, $17.50 per 100 shares. The state tax and the Federal tax amounted to 9¢ a share.

The following is a copy of Mr. Howard's account, taken from the broker's books:

WILLIAM D. HOWARD 

128 North Street, Jersey City, N. J. 

Com. at 48 

Brokerage, Buy 19June 1 Deposit 

54 4,875 00 July 1 Sold 100 sh. U. S. 

Steel Com. at 542 19June L.F. 

1 100 sh. U. S. Steel 

54 15 00 July 

1 Brokerage, Sell 69 17 50 1 

Int. on Loan 5¢ ($2,890.00) 

1 Tax: State 4, Fed. 

10 Balance Remitted 14 45 69 9 00 

2,544 05 17,475/00l L.F. 41 42.00000 68 5,475 00 17,475 00 

How much did Mr. Howard gain on this transaction?

Selling Short 

Had Mr. Howard sold 100 shares of U. S. Steel short on the market, his account would have been handled in the same way, but the results would have been different. Mr. Howard would have deposited with his broker the margin required. The broker would have sold the stock, and credited Mr. Howard's account with the amount of the sale and charged him with the brokerage and tax.

The broker would then lend the stock to Mr. Howard to sell. Later 

Mr. Howard would buy back the stock on the market and return it to the broker, and the account would be closed. 

Example. Reverse the order of the steps in the transaction and write up Mr. Howard's account for a short sale of 100 shares of U. S. Steel stock. Reversing the order means that the broker sold the stock at 

542 and that Mr. Howard bought it back at 484.

Only in transactions involving large amounts is any interest or any premium allowed by the broker to the seller who sells short.

A bull trader makes a profit on his transactions in a rising market. A bear trader makes a profit on his transactions in a falling market.

PART III ARITHMETIC IN BUSINESS

ARITHMETIC IN BUSINESS 

Arithmetic has a place and use in every kind of business, in every type of industry, and in many activities of our social order. 

Business is a form of activity which deals with the supplying of needs, or the supplying of things desired, and with the rendering of service.

The rendering of service is featured in nearly every business activity. The grocer who operates the neighborhood grocery store is not only supplying many needs but is also rendering valuable service in operating this store for the convenience of his customers. In many business activities the service element is the most important feature. The purpose of business is profit making.

Our industrial activities are many. Mining and oil production are our major extractive industries, and farming and manufacturing are our major productive industries. Other activities, such as insurance, transportation, banking, retailing, wholesaling, and commission and brokerage transactions, emphasize more directly the service feature. 

Our social order concerns itself with those religious, educational, and benevolent activities that are conducive to the betterment of society. Such activities are carried on by organizations in very much the same way as business is conducted, except that the purpose is not profit making. 

Retailing consists of purchasing in larger quantities from supply sources, and of selling in smaller quantities direct to the consumer. The retailer supplies a need and renders a service.

The retail merchant must have some cash capital with which to start a business or to purchase a business already in operation. 291

He must either own or rent a place of business where he decides to begin his undertaking. He must work out all the plans for conducting his business; open a bank account, if banking facilities are available; and then place orders, with producers and wholesalers, for the stock of goods which he is to offer for retail sale. 

The Business of Buying 

The retail merchant buys the stock of goods which he sells, from the producer, the manufacturer, the wholesaler, or the commission merchant. The retailer may, according to the nature of his business, buy at different times from any one of those mentioned or from all of them. A merchant operating a general store would draw on more different sources of supply than would a merchant who is conducting a fruit business or a butter-and-egg business. MEAT DEP

Retail Grocery.

A Retail Grocer 

Howard King is about to open a retail grocery business. He has a cash capital of $5,000, and he has rented the store building at 416 High Street, Marion, Indiana. He has opened a checking account with the Merchants National Bank of Marion, and also has had his credit rating determined by Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., New York City. Mr. King is now ready to place orders for goods. 

Orders may be given to sales representatives of the different producers or wholesalers; they may be telephoned in; or letters containing them may be written. It is better to order through a sales representative or by letter until business relations with producers and wholesalers have been established. The first orders by letter should give credit references. 

The following is a sample letter order: 

416 High Street 

Marion, Indiana

30 cases ground M. H. Coffee 

4 cases ground Black Pepper, 2 oz. 

4 cases ground Cayenne Pepper, 2 oz.

For financial reference you may apply to the Merchants National Bank of Marion, and to Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., New York City. 

Prompt shipment of this order on your usual terms is desired. 

Yours very truly, Howard King

THE BUSINESS OF SELLING 

Mr. King will find it necessary to place many orders for goods before his stock will be complete enough to supply his trade with all the commodities commonly supplied by a grocer. 

When the customer comes to the store and purchases certain articles for which he pays cash at the time of making the purchase, no order blank is filled out. When orders are telephoned in, the order blank is filled out. Also, when an order is to be filled and delivered later, the order blank is used. (See page 297.)

When a charge account is used by a customer, a carbon copy of the completed order is filed in a cabinet file employed for this purpose, where the total of each order is entered on a blank form kept for this purpose. This form is totaled for the week or month, according to the time when the customer usually settles his account.

A cash business entails less risk and less trouble for all concerned, but many dealers find it advisable to run charge accounts for customers.

"Protest and notice of dishonor waived by each maker and indorser hereof" means that, in case the note is not paid by the maker when it becomes due, the holder does not have to protest it and send notice of non-payment to the indorser in order to hold him responsible for payment of the note. 

The bank may require Mr. King to have someone else sign his note or indorse it for security; or, the bank may ask Mr. King to secure his note in some other way.

SUMMARY 

The retail grocery business is, in general, typical of any retail business. The following steps are necessary: (1) to organize the available resources; (2) to plan for the conducting of the business; (3) to invest the necessary capital and to buy the goods to be offered for sale; and (4) to sell, and also to serve in many ways, the customers on whom the business depends for success. 

Trading Statements 

To find the gross profit or the gross loss resulting from his purchases and sales, the retail merchant prepares what is commonly known as a trading statement. 

Example. A merchant's records show the following: inven tory of goods in stock at the beginning of the year, $5,742.00; purchases during the year, $18,328.60; sales during the year, $22,466.70. He paid freight amounting to $341.20 on goods. 1

purchased. Goods on hand at the end of the year inventoried $6,384.50.

The wholesale merchant buys from the producer, or manufacturer, in large quantities, and sells to the retailer in such

quantities as he needs for his trade. The wholesale merchant is called the middleman. 

The wholesale merchant, in order that he may conduct his business properly, needs sufficient capital, adequate buildings and equipment for storing and handling goods, a sales force competent to handle the trade, and an office and managerial 

Wholesale House: Packing and Shipping Department. 

staff to take care of the office routine and management of the business.

In a well-organized wholesale house there is a purchasing department, a receiving department, a sales department, a packing and shipping department, a credit department, and a billing, bookkeeping, and accounting department. Each department has its department head, who exercises general supervision over his department.

Purchase Order.

Wholesale merchants usually make arrangements, with certain producers or manufacturers, to take over a part or all of their products. When an order for goods is placed, it is usually made out on a blank for that purpose, called a purchase order. (See the illustration on page 308.)

At the time of sending in the order the person ordering the goods does not always know the prices of the articles desired.
