Wholesale merchants may aim to make a profit of 25% or more on the gross cost of the goods that they handle; however, in the pricing of goods, competition and supply and demand have to be considered. Market conditions control, to a very large extent, the pricing of goods. 

Credit 

A wholesale merchant is generally required to give credit to certain of his customers. The giving of credit entails considerable risk, and for this reason the credit department is charged with great responsibility. Since a credit business frequently results in a loss, merchants feel that they can afford to offer a discount for cash, which requires payment for goods within 10 days. Those customers who avail themselves of the full term of credit are paying for the credit privilege the amount of the cash discount. This amount is used to offset the expenses of the credit department.

Problems D 

1. One customer purchased $19,826.50 worth of goods in one year.

He availed himself of the full term of credit on each purchase, instead of taking the 2% discount for cash. How much did he pay for the credit privilege? 

2. The customer in Problem 1 made a gross profit of $6,342.60 for the year. Had he taken the 2% cash discount, how would it have affected (a) his gross profits? (b) his net profits? 

3. Are the wholesale merchant's gross profits greater if his customers do not take advantage of the cash discount? 

4. A wholesale merchant's purchases for one year amounted to $386,592.40. The average cash discount on his purchases was 21%. 

How much could be gained by buying for cash? 

5. The wholesale merchant in Problem 4 established a line of credit at his bank, and at times had his notes discounted that he might pay cash for goods purchased and so avail himself of the cash discount. To take advantage of the total cash discount on the purchases in Problem 4, he had the following notes discounted and used the proceeds to pay cash. 

How much did he gain?

(a) 90-day note for $30,000 at 6%. (b) 60-day note for $25,000 at 6%. (c) 4-month note, dated July 20 of the current year, for $40,000 at 6%. (d) 30-day note for $40,000 at 6%.

By discounting these notes, the merchant was able to pay cash for all purchases. He would gain the difference between the total cash discount on the purchases and the total bank discount on the various notes. 

Drafts 

The wholesale merchant frequently finances his transactions in this way. He draws a draft against the purchaser for the amount of the invoice, attaches the draft to the order bill of lading, and has his bank forward the draft and the order bill of lading to a bank in the community where the customer is doing business. The customer will have to accept this draft before he can secure the bill of lading with which to claim the goods when they reach the freight station in his community. 

When the draft is accepted, it is sent back through the banks to the wholesale merchant. The merchant may indorse this draft and have it discounted at his bank, in order to have additional cash available with which to buy more goods for cash, or to take advantage of cash discounts. When the draft becomes due, the drawee is expected to pay it on demand. 

Following is the typical procedure in such a transaction: (1) The order is filled, and the goods are delivered to the freight office at the shipping point. (2) The order bill of lading is issued by the freight agent for the railroad company. (3) The draft is drawn by the shipper, attached to the order bill of lading, and taken to his bank to handle. (4) The bank forwards the bill of lading, with draft attached, to a bank in the place where the purchaser of the goods (the drawee of the draft) is in business. (5) The bank receiving the draft presents it to the drawee, who accepts it. As soon as the drawee accepts the draft, the bill of lading is turned over to him and he can secure the goods from the freight office.

(6) The accepted draft may be held at the bank in the place where the drawee lives, and later collected by the bank when it becomes due. 

Or, the draft may be returned through the banks to the drawer, who is also the payee. (7) When the accepted draft is returned to the drawer, it may be discounted at a bank by the drawer (payee). In such an instance the bank discounting the draft will collect it from the drawee when the instrument becomes due. If the drawee fails to pay, then the indorser, the one for whom the draft was discounted, will have to pay.

Trading Statements

Problem E A wholesale merchant's records show the following: inventory of goods on hand at the beginning of the year, $21,378.40; purchases during the year, $118,912.25; returned purchases, $618.60; sales during the year, $147,816.90; returned sales, $588.10. He paid freight amounting to $1,188.30 on goods purchased. Goods on hand at the end of the year inventoried $22,560.70. Find this merchant's gross profit on sales.

In all of the following operations, the wholesale merchant has use for arithmetic: in billing at the different prices; in figuring trade and cash discounts; in figuring interest and bank discount; in ascertaining gross and net profit in terms of cash price, selling price, and operating expenses; in finding percentage of gross profit or net profit on cost price or on selling price. 

THE MANUFACTURER 

There must be a source of supply for the many different kinds of goods handled by the many types of merchants the country over. The manufacturer is one principal source of supply. 

Cloth, clothing, furniture, rugs, carpets, and prepared food are examples of manufactured products.

The retailer, in some cases, buys direct from the manufacturer; the wholesaler depends almost entirely on the manufacturer, or producer, for his supply of goods. 

The manufacturer buys materials in the raw, or unfinished, state, and from these materials he produces finished products, such as cloth from cotton or wool, leather from hides, shoes from leather. 

The manufacturer must have sufficient capital with which to conduct his business; he must have buildings and the necessary equipment; he must have adequate help, both skilled and unskilled. He must figure his costs of production and determine the price at which to sell his products. Here again market conditions have to be considered when articles are priced for sale. Articles priced too high are not in demand, and articles

THE MANUFACTURER 

317 priced too low are sold at a loss; therefore, the manufacturer must keep his production costs down to a level where he can sell his products at a fair margin of profit. 

Overproduction is a serious menace to favorable market conditions. For this reason some means of production control, 

Silk Manufacturing Industry: Knitting Room.

either coöperative or governmental, as a safeguard against heavy financial losses, is a justifiable reason for "Code" regulations. 

Manufacturing Costs 

Items which go into production costs are: (1) Raw materials, including buying and delivery costs. (2) Wages and salaries of those employed in production. 

Factory, or plant, overhead expenses are: (1) Rent; or, if buildings are owned, then taxes and repairs are overhead expenses. (2) Interest on capital invested and on borrowed money used in the business. (3) Fuel, light, water, and insurance. (4) Salaries of executives and office employees. 

Selling expenses include such items as: (1) Commissions paid selling agents or agencies. (2) Salaries of salesmen and their expenses. (3) Advertising in all forms. (4) Boxing, carting, and sometimes express and freight charges on manufactured goods sold and delivered. Problems F 1. During a 6-month period a hat manufacturer received orders for 

36,000 hats. He sold the hats at $30 per dozen, less the usual cash discount: 4/10, n/90. To produce these hats the manufacturing costs were as follows: raw materials, including felting fur, forms, dye, lining, binding, and band materials, $21,400; wages and salaries in production, $24,500; overhead expenses distributed, $11,600; selling expenses, $15,250. (a) Find the total cost of production and selling. (b) Find the total cost, to the manufacturer, of one dozen hats. (c) Find the gross profit, less the 4% discount, on the total sales (36,000 hats). (d) The gross profit, after the discount is taken, is what per cent of the total cost, including the cost of selling? (e) The wholesale merchant who bought the hats took the 4% discount. He sold the hats to retailers at $36 per dozen, less a 3% discount? How much did he make on each dozen hats. (f) The wholesale merchant's gross profit is what per cent of the net cost? (g) A retail merchant, who discounts his bills, sold the hats at $4 each. How much did he make on each dozen?

THE MANUFACTURER 

319 (h) This retail merchant's gross profit is what per cent of his selling price? 

2. A certain size of automobile tire was made to sell, in hundred lots, for $500 per 100 tires, less 4% discount. The manufacturing costs of 100,000 tires were as follows: raw materials, including rubber products and clay, $123,000; canvas webbing and cord products, $74,000; wages and salaries in production, $132,000; overhead expenses apportioned, $31,000; selling expenses apportioned, $24,000. (a) Find the total cost to the manufacturer. (b) Find the gross profit at the net selling price. (c) Find the per cent of gross profit on the total cost, including the selling expenses. (d) What per cent of the total cost were the labor costs (wages and salaries)? (e) Through distributing centers, the manufacturer sold the tires, in lots of 100 or more, direct to the retailer. These tires retailed for $6 each. How much did the retailer, who took advantage of the 4% discount, make on each tire sold? (f) What per cent of profit on the net cost did the retailer make on each tire that he sold for $6? (g) How much each did these tires cost to produce? (h) How much was the manufacturer's gross profit on each tire sold? (i) As a result of his experience in producing the first 100,000 tires, this same manufacturer found that he could reduce his manufacturing and selling costs 10%. Find the total cost of each lot of 100 tires produced under the new conditions. (j) For how much less could the manufacturer now sell each lot of 100 tires and still make the same percentage of gross profit on the total cost? (k) How much less would the manufacturer make on each lot of 100 tires? (1) For how much less could the retailer sell each tire and still make the same percentage of gross profit on the net cost? 

The manufacturer who can keep his production costs down can market his product at a more reasonable price and still make a fair profit. His first problem is one of production costs; his second problem is one of selling expenses, or costs of marketing his product. Usually a certain per cent of the selling price is allowed to cover the cost of selling. (m) After machinery had been installed to do the work formerly done by hand, the same number of tires could be produced at a saving of $250 per day, and 50 men were consequently thrown out of employment. Formerly it took 220 men 100 days to produce 100,000 tires; 1

with the new machinery 170 men could produce 100,000 tires in the same time. (1) Since these employees received $5 per day, how much were the labor costs reduced on 100,000 tires? (2) How much less does it cost now to produce each tire?

When men are thrown out of employment because machines are used to do their work, these men are frequently not able to secure new positions, and, as a result, they are not able to purchase many commodities. Hence, this lessening of the demand for goods (purchasing power) makes it necessary for producers to cut down on production. It is true, however, that a few men may find employment in the production of the new machines. But this number is small in comparison with the total unemployed.

6. (a) In Problem 1, what per cent of the total selling price of the hats, before the discount is taken, are the total selling expenses? (The gross selling price is: 36,000 ÷ 12 × $30. The selling expenses are $15,250.) (b) In Problem 2, find what per cent the selling expenses are of the total manufacturing costs, including the selling expenses. (Selling expenses ÷ total manufacturing costs result.) 

THE COMMISSION BUSINESS 

A very large part of the world's business is conducted on a commission basis. The commission business is confined almost entirely to the operations of buying and selling. Insurance, real estate, stock and bond transactions, distribution of the different kinds of manufactured merchandise, as well as products of the soil-these are the major divisions in this field of business activity.

A commission agent transacts for some other party the business of buying or selling; for this service he is paid a certain percentage of the amount involved in the transaction. This percentage is his commission.

Example. A real estate agent sold a house and lot, for the owner, for $12,000. The agent charged 5% of the $12,000, or $600, for making the sale. The owner received $11,400, less the lawyer's charges for drawing up the contract of sale and making out the deed of conveyance.

Buying on Commission 

Many articles are sold through commission merchants. Likewise, many commodities are purchased through such merchants. 

PINELLAS ORANGES PRODUCE OF ALIFORNIA SLOWERS EXCHANG FRUIT EVCANC 

Commission House: Fruit and Vegetable Market. 

To illustrate, a certain milling company may desire to buy a quantity of wheat to make into flour. The company will place an order with some grain broker to buy on a particular grain market-such as Minneapolis or Duluth-the quantity desired.

The broker will buy the wheat and charge a commission for his services. The commission for buying or selling grain is usually a certain amount per bushel-for example, é per bushel. Problems G To find the total cost of a purchase and to prepare an account purchase.

When an order to purchase goods is placed with a commission merchant, it is not always the practice to remit the approximate amount of the account purchase. Prepare an account purchase for this transaction.

Miners and coal merchants no longer use the long ton (2,240 lb.). Railroad companies, however, continue to use it. 

Selling on Commission

The commission merchant renders a very valuable service by selling on established markets various kinds of goods for the producer. One commission merchant can serve a number of producers. Moreover, as a result of his experience and facilities, he can in most cases market products to better advantage than could the producer himself.

In the transaction the commission merchant, or broker, acts for a party known as the principal. 

The compensation paid the agent, or broker, is usually a certain percentage of the amount of the transaction, or a certain price per unit of weight or measure. The insurance agent receives as his compensation a portion of the premium paid over a period of time; the selling agent receives a percentage of his sales; and the stockbroker charges a certain per cent of the par value of the stock or a certain price per share according to the market value of the stock.

The fundamental principles of charging commission-at a certain price per unit of weight or measure (that is, 2¢ per bushel; 1¢ per pound; 25¢ per barrel) or at a certain per cent of the selling price or of the cost price-apply to all commission problems. There are, however, many different applications of these fundamental principles, as the following problems illustrate.

THE BANKING BUSINESS 

The banker deals in money. He accepts money on deposit from people who wish to avail themselves of banking facilities, either for checking purposes or for safe-keeping and interest income. 

The banker, in turn, lends or invests certain portions of the deposits in his bank, and for these loans and investments he 

By Ewing Galloway, N. Y

Modern Banking Establishment. 

receives a higher rate of interest than he pays to his depositors. 

This interest margin is the bank's principal source of income with which to meet operating expenses and to pay dividends on the capital stock belonging to stockholders, or owners, of the bank. 

National banks are required by law to keep from 7% to 13% of the money on deposit available to pay checks and to meet current demands. This provision permits the banks to make

loans on and invest in approved securities up to the amount of 

87% to 93% of their depositors' money. (See section on "Reserves," in following text.)

The bank has, moreover, other sources of income: rent from safety deposit boxes; collection and exchange charges; and rent from income property owned by the bank, or from property taken over by foreclosure proceeding. 

Starting a Bank

To start a national bank, there must be at least five interested persons with sufficient capital to subscribe as stockholders and become directors of the bank. 

The first step in organizing a bank is to apply to the Federal 

Department of Banking, Washington, D. C., for a charter.

Before this charter is granted, banking officials employed by the Department of Banking will investigate the merits of the application and determine if another bank, or any bank, should be started at this location. When the charter is granted, the bank may be opened for business.

In the case of state banks and trust companies, the organizing procedure is very much the same, except that the application for a charter is sent to the state department of banking in the state where the bank is to be located. State banks are supervised by the state departments of banking; national banks are supervised by the Comptroller of the Currency in the Treasury Department. 

Every national bank is required to be a member of the Federal 

Reserve System.

Capital. The amount of capital required to open a national bank is: in a place of less than 6,000 population, a minimum capital of $50,000 is required; in a place of over 6,000, a minimum capital of $100,000 is required.

Reserves. Every member bank of the Federal Reserve System is required by law to maintain on deposit with the Federal Reserve bank of its district an actual net balance equal to 3% of its time deposits, and, in addition to this, from 7% to 13% of its demand deposits (checking accounts). The amount of the

demand deposit reserve depends upon the size of the town or city in which the bank is located: in small towns, 7% is required; in medium-size cities, 10%; and in large cities, 13%. 

Deposit insurance. At the present time, bank deposits are insured by the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation. This insurance is limited to the deposits of banks qualifying under the Banking Act of 1933.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was brought into existence with the enactment of the Banking Act of 1933. This 

Corporation is managed by the Comptroller of the Currency and two citizens appointed by the President of the United States. 

Through the purchase of stock of the Corporation, banks secure insurance for the accounts of their customers. 

Definite information concerning banking operations and detailed information covering banking transactions can be secured at any commercial bank.

Reconciling a Bank Balance

When the depositor receives from his bank a statement of his account, it shows his last balance, or the balance of his account at the time this statement was sent out. The depositor should compare the bank's balance with the balance in his check book. 

Usually there is a difference in the balances. Such discrepancies may result from one or more of the following causes:

When the bank's account does not show checks, issued by the depositor, which are still outstanding. 

When the depositor's check book record does not show: (a) Interest allowed by the bank on the depositor's balance. (b) Collection fees charged by the bank on collections made for the depositor, or exchange fees. (c) Proceeds of collections placed to the depositor's credit. (d) Notes given and drafts accepted by the depositor which are payable at the bank and are paid and charged to the depositor's account. (e) Deposits not added to the balance in the depositor's check book. (f) Checks accepted for deposit which were dishonored and afterwards deducted from the depositor's account. (g) Charges made by the bank for handling the depositor's account (cashing checks, and so on).

To reconcile a bank balance. A bank balance may be recon ciled as follows: 

1. Deduct from the bank's balance the total of the outstanding checks. 

2. Deduct from the check book balance the total of all items, appearing in the bank's statement against the depositor's account, which have not already been deducted. 

3. Compare the two final balances. 

If no error has been made, the depositor's balance (2) will agree with the bank's balance (1).

No. 128, for $82.50, and No. 132, for $41.30-were still outstanding. (a) Prepare a reconciliation statement for Mr. Forbes. (b) Assume that the bank allowed Mr. Forbes $1.56 interest on daily balances for the month. How would this item affect the reconciliation? (c) If, on October 31, Mr. Forbes made a deposit of $216.80 too late in the afternoon for the amount to appear in the bank statement, how would this deposit affect the reconciliation? 

FARMING 

Many times more people are engaged in farming or in agricultural occupations of all kinds than in any other single industry. 

In 1930, according to the Federal census, the total farm population was 30,447,550, and there were, in addition, 10,482,323 persons engaged in agricultural occupations. The total population of the United States in 1930 was 122,775,046.

Therefore, approximately 35% of our entire population gain their livelihood from the farm. And, of the entire number of people engaged in gainful occupations, about 40% are engaged in agriculture.

The farm is the main source of supply of food and other essential products. The figures given above indicate the relative importance of the farm in our great economic order. The 

United States is essentially an agricultural country, and its economic progress is most likely to be in direct proportion to the economic stability of the farmer. 

Farm Financing

Financing farm projects is the cause of much concern to farmers; at times failure in being able to finance production causes much loss. The farmer has many expenses to meet in the course of production. In the case of fruits, grains, vegetables, or livestock, he has to wait some time before he has anything to market; then, the expense of harvesting and of preparing for market has to be paid before he secures any returns from his products.

FARMING 

343 The farmer who would succeed, other things being equal, must have sufficient capital with which to finance his activities, or he must have tangible assets available to secure the loan of money that he can use for financing purposes. 

Farming: Cutting and Binding Oats. 

The successful farmer is a good planner, he is a good manager, and he knows how to operate efficiently.

Equipment 

The farmer must either own or rent the land that he is to cultivate; he must own or have the use of the necessary machinery and tools; he must have adequate help to perform the physical operations of farming; he must have the necessary seed and fertilizer; and he must have adequate facilities in the way of buildings and accessories. It can thus be seen that the farmer's equipment is extensive, that his capital outlay is considerable,

that his responsibility is weighty; therefore, training and experience are essential to his success. 

Production and Marketing 

Farming today involves two major problems. The first is concerned with production; the second, with marketing. The farmer may be skilled in producing, but this facility avails him little if he cannot find a good market for his produce. These two phases of farming merit intensive study. 

Most states provide means for training the prospective farmer, or agriculturist, for his chosen vocation. In state universities throughout the country many experimental stations are operated in the interests of the farmer and farming. Many schools and colleges offer agricultural courses. As a result of all this activity, agriculture has in recent years developed considerably along scientific lines.

Marketing has received much attention in recent years. Many agencies have been set up to study and improve market conditions. Some of these activities are fostered by the Federal 

Government and some by the individual states. Private interests have also, through coöperative agencies, endeavored to improve market conditions. 

Coöperative Marketing

Coöperative marketing is one of the more recent innovations in progressive agriculture. Organizations of interested persons are formed, with proper leadership, and are known as associations for coöperative marketing. As soon as such an organization is completed, the next step is usually to launch a membership campaign. Each member pays a membership fee or buys shares of stock in the association, and is entitled to the benefits resulting from the organization. When the plans have been completed, a board of governors, or directors, and the officers are elected. 

Generally a man trained and experienced in marketing is selected as manager for the association.

Coöperative marketing associations are found in sections where production of certain products is extensively carried on;

FARMING 

345 for example, fruit growers' associations, in the fruit-growing states, such as California and some of the southern states; grain alliances, in the grain-growing states of the Middle West; dairy leagues, in many sections where dairy products are marketed extensively. 

The advantages of coöperative marketing are many. Such associations set up standards for products, all of which, in order to be marketed, must be carefully graded, packed, and labeled according to the regulations of the association. This practice insures protection for both producer and buyer. The producer's products are made marketable in the most approved way, and the seller is protected in buying graded and labeled products. 

When labeled and advertised products are not kept up to standard, the market for them soon disappears. The consumer must be satisfied. 

Sunkist oranges, Diamond walnuts, and Blue Goose grape fruit are examples of labeled products. There are many others. 

The expenses of a coöperative marketing association are paid from membership fees and from small commissions charged members for handling their products. Through coöperative agencies, market conditions can be more efficiently considered, and better prices and steadier outlets, secured. 

Operating in many sections are coöperative buying associations. Through its manager, such an organization buys, direct from producers, large quantities of seed, fertilizer, containers, feed, and so on, and distributes these articles, in smaller quantities, among the members of the association. This coöperative purchasing makes it possible for the individual user to secure equipment at a much lower price than he would otherwise have to pay.

COÖPERATIVE MARKETING OF GRAIN 

In a certain grain-growing section of the United States, a coöperative marketing association was formed. One hundred grain growers in that section joined the organization. The membership fee was $10, and each member was required to buy 5 shares of stock at $50 per share.

The sum of $1,000, collected from membership fees, was put in a special fund to be used only when authorized by a vote of the stockholders. The amount received from the sale of stock, $25,000, was used to buy land, to erect a storage elevator, and to buy necessary equipment. The stockholders elected from among their number a board of directors, who selected a manager to take charge of the business of the association. 

The association charged each member 2¢ per bushel for handling grain and ¢ per bushel a month storage after the first 30 days. Operating plans were worked out, and selling prices were agreed upon by the board of directors. 

The first year the business showed gross earnings of $7,850; the operating expenses were $5,725. The directors paid a dividend of 6% on the capital stock of $25,000, and added $625 to the special fund, which then totaled $1,625. 

The foregoing illustration is typical of the way in which a well-managed coöperative association should succeed. Storing the grain at a very small charge makes it possible for the producer to hold and then later to sell and ship when market conditions are most favorable. The law of supply and demand can be taken into account. When the supply of any product offered on the market is greater than the demand, the price of the product drops. 

Hence, under present conditions, marketing requires much care and study, and is of special concern to the producer. Problems K

Dairy Farming

The dairy farmer produces milk and milk products. He may sell his milk to creameries, for the production of butter products; to factories, for the production of cheese products; or to milk distributors, for sale from producers to consumers. Some dairy farmers sell and deliver milk direct to consumers, and some make butter and other products which they sell. 

MILK CONTENT 

Pure milk is composed of the following elements, in proportions approximately as listed below:

Mineral Matter. 

Water...

Pure Milk. 

Per Cent 3.5 4.5 3.0 0.7 0.7 87.5 100.0

The price paid for milk varies in proportion to the amount of butterfat in the milk content. Some milk tests high in butterfat; some, low.

The butterfat content of milk varies from less than 3% up to 5%. In most localities 3.6% is considered standard. 

Dairy Farming: Milking Cows by Means of Electrically Operated Machines. 

Milk dealers who purchase milk for various purposes usually test the butterfat content and pay accordingly for the milk. 

Milk with a high butterfat content brings the best price, since richer milk is better for cream and butter products. in bulk quantities is usually sold by the hundredweight. Problems L

BUDGET MAKING 

Every person who has any financial matters to handle should prepare a budget of expenses based on a definite or probable income. The person who works on a specified salary, or the person who owns income-producing securities on which the income, in the form of interest or dividends, is definite, or who owns property from which the rental income is definite, can plan his budget from month to month or by the year. The person whose income is indefinite can plan only for probable expenses and possible savings, based on past experience. Problems M 1. A married man with two children draws a salary of $3,000.00 a year. 

He plans a budget of expenses as follows: rent, $600.00; food, $600.00; gas and electricity, $75.00; fuel, $135.00; clothing, $300.00; doctor and dentist, $100.00; miscellaneous expenses, $150.00; insurance, $140.00.

He wishes to save the amount remaining. (a) How much does he plan to save during the year?

This man's expenses were as follows: rent, which was reduced $5.00 per month for the last 6 months, $570.00; food, $685.00; gas and electricity, $81.60; fuel, $137.50; clothing, $368.40; doctor and dentist, $40.00; insurance, $140.00; miscellaneous, $128.75. (b) Reconcile his actual expenses with his budget plans and find how much he actually saved. 

2. A married man with a family has an income of $4,000.00 a year.

BUYING 

It is one thing to make money and quite another thing to use it wisely. Knowing what to buy, when to buy, and how to buy 

Courtesy Cooley Realty Co., Mount Vernon, N. Y.

Buying a Home. 

is fundamental to thrift. To insure financial success, not only is it necessary to accumulate money or property, or both, but

it is also necessary to use this money and property to the greatest advantage.

Buying a Home 

There are times when rents are low and a person can rent living quarters for less than it would cost him to own and maintain a home. Moreover, a person's business may be such that it requires him to move about from place to place and hence he cannot own a home to advantage.

On the other hand, there is much to be said in favor of home owning. It results in greater interest in the home and means much to family life; it causes the owner to take more interest in civic affairs.

Problems N 1. William J. Manker is paying $90.00 per month for rent in a 6-room house. He has a chance to buy a 6-room house for $8,500.00, on the following terms: cash, $1,500.00; first mortgage for $5,000.00 at 6%; second mortgage for $2,000.00 at 6%, to be amortized semiannually over a period of 4 years. To amortize the second mortgage, he is to pay $250.00 on the principal and interest due on the balance for the 

6-month period preceding.

The taxes on this house are $184.50 per year. The water rate is $3.00 per quarter. It is estimated that the cost of keeping the house in repair will be $170.00 (2%). The insurance is $30.00 for 3 years. (a) Would you advise Mr. Manker to buy this house? Show your figures, including interest at 6% on the $8,500.00. (b) How much would Mr. Manker have to pay on the second mortgage during the 4-year period? 

2. A family is renting a house for $100.00 per month. This house is offered for sale at $12,000.00. There is a mortgage for $6,000.00 at 6% on the property. This mortgage has 3 years to run and may be renewed. 

The expenses on the house are: taxes, $263.70; insurance, $45.00 for 

3 years; water rate, about $20.00 a year; estimated cost of upkeep and repairs, $250.00. It will take $6,000.00 cash to buy this house. The house is in a good location, and its value is not likely to depreciate. 

The family has the $6,000.00 invested in building and loan stock, where the money is earning 6% interest. (a) Would you advise the family to buy this house? Show your figures.

Buying a Store Building

A merchant must either rent a store or own the building in which he wishes to operate his business. Whether it is a better plan for him to rent or to buy is a question many a merchant has to decide. 

Chain store operators usually rent store units, because they wish to be free to change location or possibly even to close a particular store in case it does not prove to be a paying investment. 

In deciding this question of renting or buying, there are many points to consider. The following problem illustrates some of them.

Problem O Mr. Bantz, who is a dry goods merchant, is paying $3,000 a year for rent in the first floor of a building on the main business street of his city.

This building is three stories high. The second floor rents for $90 per month; the third floor, for $75. Both floors are used for lodge purposes, and the rental income is reasonably sure. 

This building and lot can be purchased for $45,000 if the buyer pays $20,000 in cash and gives a purchase money mortgage on the property for $25,000 at 6% for 5 years. The taxes on the property are $850 a year; the insurance is $40 per year on a 3-year policy; the other carrying charges amount to about $1,000 per year.

Mr. Bantz considers his money worth 8% interest to him in his business. 

Would you advise him to buy this building and lot? Show your figures. 

Your advice must take the following points into account: Is the building likely to depreciate in value? How long has it been built? Is it modern? Moreover, Mr. Bantz has been established in this building for a long time. The building is on the market to be sold. Mr. Bantz can renew his lease for only one year. He may be forced to move to another less desirable location.

Buying on the Deferred Payment Plan 

Buying on the deferred payment plan has become a common practice. Today many articles in common use are sold in this way and paid for in weekly or monthly installments. 

When he buys in this way, the purchaser has to pay more for the article than he would pay if he were buying for cash. The purchaser can justify buying on the deferred payment plan if he can show how much he will save by having the use of the article during that time, and how much inconvenience or loss he would suffer by not having the article. He must also consider whether or not he could make other less costly plans and still arrive at the same end. There are many pro's and con's to the deferred payment plan for buying.

The following is the usual way of finding the amount of each deferred payment. Add to the amount unpaid the interest for the full time at the rate charged, usually 6%, and then divide the whole amount by the number of payments. 

Example. An article has been sold for $800. The amount unpaid is $600. The installments are to be paid monthly, for 12 months, at 6% interest. What is the amount of each payment? $36, interest $600 at 6% for 1 year $600 + $36 $636 12 (12 months)

Sometimes, in addition to 

$636, whole amount $53, amount of each payment the interest, a financing charge is made, as in Problem 3 on page 357.

It can be seen that the purchaser is actually paying more than 12% interest, since at the end of the first 6 months he has paid one half of the amount on which he is paying interest for the full time.

INVESTMENTS 

To make money and to save money require a type of ability possessed by many, but to invest money wisely requires experience and judgment. Even then mistakes are sometimes made. 

High interest rates and large dividends are attractive features to investment seekers. However, too often when rates of interest or dividends are high, securities are correspondingly unsound. 

Usually, the sounder the security, the lower the interest rate. 

Hence some investors are content to invest in United States Government bonds although the interest return is comparatively low. 

Among the various sources of interest income are the following: (1) First mortages on real estate form one source of income. These usually pay from 5% to 7%, according to the location of the real estate and conditions on the money market. The usual rate is 6%. (2) First mortgage certificates are issued by mortgage companies. 

These certificates are secured by a first mortgage on real estate and are guaranteed by a mortgage company. 

The interest rate on these certificates is usually 5% or 5%, or sometimes 6% in places where the legal rate of interest is more than 6%, as it is in California and other western states. (3) Bonds of all types bear interest at different rates. A corporation bond is secured by a mortgage on the property of the organization issuing the bond. In the case of municipal bonds-which include any bonds issued by the Federal Government, or by a state, county, city, town, village, or school district unit-the taxable property within the political unit issuing the bond is security for the bond. (4) The principal source of dividends may be found in the different kinds of stock issued by business corporations, such as banks, insurance companies, railroad companies, manufacturing companies, and so on. 

Among the numerous business projects which attract investors are real estate developments, sales promotions, and many others.

INVESTMENTS 

359 Problems Q Investing in interest income sources. 

1. Henry Morris bought fifty $500 United States Government bonds, paying 4% interest, at 102. (a) With brokerage at 1%, how much did he pay for the bonds? (b) Since the interest was paid semiannually, how much did he receive each time the interest was paid? (c) What rate of interest income does this investment, not including brokerage, pay to the purchaser? (d) How does the interest return on these bonds compare with 4% interest, credited to an account semiannually, on savings deposited in a savings bank? (e) What is the difference between a registered bond and a coupon bond?

(b) How much was the dividend for the second year? (c) What rate of interest income did Mr. Hooker realize on his investment during the first year? (Add brokerage to find total cost, or total amount invested.) (d) What rate of interest did he realize during the second year? (e) Assume that Mr. Hooker had sold his stock after the price dropped to 471. With brokerage at $15 per 100 shares and Federal tax of 5¢ per share, how much would he have lost?

PROFITS IN PARTNERSHIPS 

The profits resulting from a business operated by a firm organized as a partnership may be distributed in any one of a number of different ways. Unless the profits are to be divided equally

among the partners, they must agree at the time of entering into their partnership how the profit or loss shall be shared. 

The following problems illustrate different methods of distributing profits in partnerships.

Problems R 

Sharing profits and losses equally. 

1. Moor and Finch are partners in a real estate business. Moor has invested $1,500.00, and Finch has invested $1,500.00. They agree to share profits and losses equally. Their gross commissions for the year amount to $9,640.60, and their operating expenses are $2,812.40. Find each partner's share of the profit. 

Sharing profits and losses according to ratios agreed upon. 

2. Loomis and Kenedy were partners in a grocery business. Loomis invested $12,000.00; Kenedy invested $8,000.00. They agreed to share the net profit in proportion to the capital each invested. They were to share losses equally. The net profit for the year was $7,428.45. Find each partner's share. 

Sharing profits and losses in proportion to the capital ratio at the beginning of a specified period. 

3. Cramer and Cohn are partners in a furniture business. They have agreed to share profits and losses in proportion to their capital ratio at the beginning of the year. Cramer's capital is $15,000.00 and 

Cohn's is $25,000.00. The gross profit for the year is $21,280.60, and the expenses are $9,120.20. After finding the net profit, determine how much each partner should receive. 

Sharing profits and losses in proportion to the average investment. 

4. Pratt and Johnson enter into a partnership to conduct an amusement park. They agree to divide the profits and losses according to the average investment—that is, each partner's average investment for a year is the basis for his share of the profit or loss for the year. 

On January 1 Pratt invests $4,000, and on July 1 of the same year he invests $1,000 additional. On January 1 Johnson invests $5,000, and on April 1 he increases his investment by $2,000. The net profit for the year is $8,800. Find each partner's share of this profit. $4,000+ $1,000 $24,000, equivalent amount for 1 month $5,000, investment July 1 $4,000 × 6 $5,000 × 6 $30,000, equivalent amount for 1 month $54,000, total equivalent amount $54,000 ÷ 12 $5,000 × 3 $24,000+ $30,000 

$4,500, Pratt's average investment $15,000, equivalent amount for 1 month $5,000 + $2,000 = $7,000, investment April 1

363 $7,000 × 9 $15,000+ $63,000 $78,000 ÷ 12 $63,000, equivalent amount for 1 month $78,000, total equivalent amount $6,500, Johnson's average investment

Sharing profits and losses in proportion to the ratio agreed upon, after interest is allowed on investments. 

5. Kuhn and Hart invested $30,000 and $20,000, respectively, in a wholesale cheese business. The partners agreed to share the profits and losses equally after each partner was allowed 6% interest on his investment. At the end of the year the net profit was $7,800. What was each partner's share of the net earnings?

FIGURES IN USE 

A figure has meaning only as it relates to some object or to some unit of weight or measure. The figure 12 standing alone is meaningless. However, 12 apples means that the object is apples, and that 12 is the number of apples. Likewise, in the expression 12 inches, the inch is a unit of measure, and 12 indicates the number of inch units.

Therefore, when we work with figures, we use them in terms of quantities and values-that is, we use them in a related, or associated, way.

Weights and measures are determined by the use of standard units, which are fixed by law. These standard units have specific names; for example, foot, pound, quart, and so on.

TABLES OF MEASURE Linear Measure

The units in linear measure represent length only, and are used to measure distance or to measure the length, width, or thickness of an object. The unit of length used as the standard in the United States is the standard yard. 365

The units comprising what was known as "surveyors' linear measure" are not used to any extent at the present time. Surveyors now use a steel tape, divided into feet, into tenths of a foot, and sometimes into inches.

A square is 10 ft. x 10 ft., or 100 sq. ft. This unit is commonly used in figuring for roofing material. 

Square measure is used to measure surfaces. The number of units of square measure in a surface is its area. For example, a table 4 feet long and 4 feet wide, or 4 feet square, has in its surface 4 ft. X 4 ft., or 16 sq. ft. (4 X 4 = 16). 

Cubic Measure

1 cubic foot (cu. ft.) 

1 cubic yard (cu. yd.) 

1 perch (P.) 

1 cord (cd.)

Units of volume, like the cubic foot and the cubic yard, are cubes of units of length. For example, an object 3 ft. X 3 ft. X 

3 ft. is equal in volume to 27 cu. ft. (3 × 3 × 3 = 27). 

Cubic measure is used to measure solids, as well as the contents, or capacity, of bins, tanks, and the like. The number of units of cubic measure contained in a solid makes up its solid contents, or its volume.

The perch, used in measuring stone or masonry, varies in different sections of the United States, but is usually considered

TABLES OF TIME

52 weeks 

1 year (yr.) 12 months 1 year 

365 days 1 year 

366 days 1 leap year 

100 years 1 century 

The exact year is 365 days, 5 hours, 48 minutes, and 49.7 seconds; or about 365 days. Therefore, every fourth year is 

366 days long. The extra day is added to the month of February, and that year is called leap year. Those years with numbers divisible by 4 are leap years; for example, 1924, 1928, 1932.

Years marking the completion of a century, such as 1800 and 1900, are not leap years unless their numbers are divisible by 400, because the year is a little short of 365 days. The year 2000 will be a leap year.
